The 5 players Real Madrid let go in the summer – & how they’re faring elsewhere

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Real Madrid had a busy summer transfer window preparing for Jose Mourinho’s return to the club, but they also let go of some players who didn’t fit into the plans anymore.

There were some big names cut adrift as well, with two first-team players reaching the end of their contracts, one more having his ripped up ahead of schedule, and another two being sold.

Here’s how they’ve fared at their new clubs so far.

Ceballos was supposed to be under contract with Madrid this season before becoming a free agent next summer, but it was agreed for him to move on this year instead.

It took a while for the midfielder to land at a new club. His exit from Madrid was announced in late June, but it wasn’t until the start of September that he officially signed for Real Betis.

Quite why it took so long is a bit of a mystery when it was always anticipated that Ceballos would go back to Betis one day, after originally leaving them for Madrid in 2017.

Ceballos hasn’t exactly hit the ground running back at Betis. His first five appearances were all off the bench and he was taken off at half time after his first start against Barcelona.

He is starting to feature more often from the first minute, including against Borussia Dortmund in the Champions League recently, but hasn’t completed 90 minutes yet.

It’s a case of building up his match fitness after missing pre-season. He also has tough competition for places in the Betis midfield, but will be backing himself for a better run of form after the international break.

After five years of service, the last three of which were affected by injuries, Madrid released Alaba in the summer.

He became a marquee signing for Serie A side Udinese in September, signing a contract to last until the end of the season.

He made his debut off the bench against Torino in October before starting at centre-back six days later against Lecce.

Since then, the Austrian has succumbed to a calf injury, leaving him stuck on two appearances for his new club so far. He was on the losing side in both.

Udinese are bottom of the Serie A table as things stand, which is quite a fall from the levels Alaba is used to playing at with Bayern Munich and Madrid.

The most emotional of all the Madrid departures this summer was Carvajal’s. A product of the club’s youth system, he spent 13 years in the first team after returning from Bayer Leverkusen and eventually ended up as captain.

Madrid decided not to renew the right-back’s contract when it expired and he remained a free agent until mid-October.

After a bit of will-they-won’t-they, fellow capital club Getafe signed Carvajal on a contract until the end of the season.

He went straight into their starting lineup for their game against Rayo Vallecano, the first of three consecutive starts in La Liga. He got booked in the first two.

Carvajal completed his move too late to be included in Getafe’s Conference League squad for the league phase and sat out their Copa del Rey win over sixth-tier side UB Lebrijana.

Garcia got three years back at Madrid after re-joining from Rayo Vallecano in 2023, before being sold to Real Betis for €4m in July.

As he’d have hoped, it’s given him the chance to become a more regular starter in La Liga. He started 10 league games for Madrid last season and is already on nine for Betis this season.

The left-back assisted the winning goal against Valencia on his second appearance for Betis. He also set one up in the Champions League against Porto.

Garcia earned a first Spain call-up in three years in October, playing a full 90 minutes against the Czech Republic.

Betis must be looking at that €4m spend as a bargain. Madrid might be interested in how he’s doing too – they’re entitled to 50% of any future sale.

For a fleeting moment, it looked like Garcia was going to break through at the Bernabeu. He did well at the Club World Cup last summer and went on to score eight goals last season.

But regular first-team football was just beyond him. Thus, he opted to follow Alvaro Arbeloa to Fulham in a €40m deal in August.

Garcia scored on his Premier League debut against Chelsea and then against AFC Wimbledon in the Carabao Cup a few days later, but has drawn a blank from his next 11 appearances.

He remains a regular starter for Fulham up front, but is emblematic of a team that struggles to convert their chances.

Bitcoin price prediction: JPMorgan’s $150K bull case for 2030 vs. $84,000 resistance

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The crypto market feels cautious, with traders selling rallies instead of chasing them. Bitcoin sits at the center of that mood after a new forecast argued it may stay below $150,000 through 2030.

The call lands as Bitcoin trades far below its record high and just above a key support level. Any Bitcoin price prediction for the coming weeks starts with whether buyers can defend that floor.

Traders who weigh long-term forecasts against short-term swings also watch early-stage projects. One of them is MEMASA ($MEMASA), an upcoming AI trading app that lets users research and trade crypto through a chat.

A recent Motley Fool analysis on Yahoo Finance argues Bitcoin will likely stay below $150,000 through the end of the decade.

Author Dominic Basulto says the $1 million targets popular among Bitcoin bulls should be shelved for now.

His math leans on JPMorgan Asset Management, which expects Bitcoin to beat stocks, bonds and gold over the next decade.

The firm’s bull case assumes yearly growth near 17%, which would land Bitcoin close to that ceiling on the same timeline.

Still, JPMorgan warns the ride will come with extreme volatility, and Bitcoin has lost about 40% over the past year. That slump explains why even Strategy’s Michael Saylor has hinted that a return to six figures may prove difficult.

Bitcoin now trades near $74,800, down about 10% over the past month. The nearest strong support sits near $73,500, while resistance stands much higher at about $84,400. Technical indicators lean bearish, so a clean break below support could open a path toward $70,000.

For any near-term Bitcoin price prediction, reclaiming $84,400 would be the first sign that buyers are back in control. Long-term forecasts like JPMorgan’s leave traders with plenty of questions, and some new tools aim to answer them faster.

MEMASA is an upcoming AI crypto trading app that lets users analyze markets, check token risks, and trade on-chain by chatting with an AI assistant.

The app tackles problems familiar to crypto traders: too many charts, scattered market data, scam tokens, and complicated interfaces. MEMASA plans to combine market intelligence, risk checks, automated strategies, and trading in one self-custodial app.

If the crypto market enters another bullish phase, growing interest in altcoins could bring more people into trading.

MEMASA aims to serve that audience by making market research and trading as simple as a conversation. Its potential appeal extends from beginners struggling with trading interfaces to experienced traders who want faster access to market insights.

Can $MEMASA benefit from the next crypto rally?

$MEMASA is the utility token powering the MEMASA ecosystem. Its planned uses include premium AI intelligence, trading fee discounts, marketplace payments, and staking for analysts and AI agents.

The token’s potential derives from stronger sentiment across the crypto market and growing demand within the MEMASA ecosystem. If the app attracts a wide audience, it might drive the demand for $MEMASA.

MEMASA is currently offering early access to $MEMASA through an on-chain early sale, with prices below the project’s anticipated public listing price. Earlier stages offer lower prices, while later stages provide faster token unlocks.

A Bitcoin rally could create favorable conditions for MEMASA’s debut. The bigger test will be whether the app delivers on its promise to simplify crypto trading and attracts enough users to build lasting demand for its token.

Bitcoin’s next test is the support near $73,500. Holding it keeps a recovery toward $84,400 in play, while a break could send the price toward $70,000.

The long view looks calmer, as JPMorgan’s bull case still points to about $150,000 by the end of the decade, though with sharp swings along the way.

MEMASA faces a different test, as it needs to turn an AI trading concept into an app people use regularly. If it succeeds, demand for its utility token could grow with the ecosystem, and today’s $0.0155 sale price would become its reference point.

Both Bitcoin’s path and MEMASA’s outcome remain uncertain, and higher potential comes with higher risk.

Dresden AI chipmaker Sparxity closes Series A, taking funding to $90M

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Sparxity, a Dresden company that builds computer chips and systems for AI, has closed its Series A funding round. The round brings the company’s total raised to $90M.

Yet Sparxity did not say how much of that came from the Series A.

ICM HPQC Fund, which invests in computing and quantum infrastructure, led the round. Lockheed Martin Ventures, chipmaker GlobalFoundries, GS Futures, Cloudberry Ventures, TGFS and the EIC Fund also took part.

Angel investors include Richard Socher, founder of Recursive Superintelligence, and Nancy Xu, whose start-up Moonhub was bought by Salesforce.

The company will use the money to develop chips for what it calls dynamic sparsity. Many AI models only use a small part of their network for each task.

Sparxity’s chips are designed to skip the parts that are not needed, which the company says saves time and energy. It is aiming for AI inference, the stage where a trained model answers requests, up to 44 times faster in very sparse models.

The company was founded in 2021 as SpiNNcloud, a spin-off from TU Dresden. It was built on SpiNNaker2, a chip inspired by how the brain works. Last month, it changed its name to Sparxity to reflect its new focus on sparse computing.

Sparxity said it has made more than 70,000 chips across three chip designs and installed 12 racks of its systems. It has more than 30 customers, including some of the world’s largest supercomputing centres, and its revenue quadrupled between 2024 and 2025.

Its current systems are used for scientific computing as well as AI. On selected tasks, the company says they run some calculations 10 times faster and uses 18 times less energy for certain machine learning work.

Hector Gonzalez, Sparxity’s co-founder and chief executive, said the money will help data centres move from power-hungry designs to setups with chips built for sparse AI.

“Sparxity has demonstrated the ability to manufacture its technology and deploy systems with major customers,” said Matthew Gould of ICM HPQC Fund.

The funding follows two recent deals. In October, Sparxity installed a system worth several million dollars at the Jülich Supercomputing Centre in Germany, home of JUPITER, Europe’s first exascale supercomputer.

Last week, it signed a letter of intent with T-Systems, the business arm of Deutsche Telekom, to bring its technology into T-Systems’ data centres.

“We are looking forward to having Sparxity on board in our AI factory soon,” said Ferri Abolhassan, chief executive of T-Systems.

Sparxity has also received EU support in the past. In 2025, it was picked for the European Innovation Council’s accelerator programme, which gave it €10m in grants and equity.

I am the Editor in Chief for TNW, covering technology not as a parade of launches and valuations, but as a system of influence, persuasion, (show all) I am the Editor in Chief for TNW, covering technology not as a parade of launches and valuations, but as a system of influence, persuasion, and change. I write about startups, venture capital, digital policy, and Europe ecosystem, with an eye on the larger story beneath them: who gets to build the future, who profits from it, and how Europe is learning to speak in a louder voice of its own. Before moving into senior editorial leadership, I've built my career for over +10 years across journalism, storytelling, content strategy, SEO, and digital publishing, with experience in SaaS, hospitality, art, and culture.

Jon Miller On AI And The Future Of Marketing Automation via @sejournal, @hethr_campbell

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Is your marketing automation platform helping you grow, or making it harder to adapt? Jon Miller, co-founder of Marketo, Engagio, and Phave, joined me on the SEJ Podcast to discuss where he believes legacy marketing systems fall short and how AI could change the way B2B teams work.

In our conversation, Miller explained why individual leads and company accounts can miss the complexity of buying groups, how campaign queues and extra tools can signal gaps in your platform, and the difference between AI that builds rules-based workflows and AI that makes decisions. He also shares why marketers should review renewal dates early and think carefully before committing to a multi-year contract.

[00:40] Meet Jon Miller [01:26] Why Miller Built Phave [04:25] Rethinking The B2B Marketing Playbook [06:11] Moving From Rules To AI Reasoning [07:58] Why Buying Groups Matter [09:40] Signs Your Marketing Stack Is Holding You Back [12:47] AI That Builds Automation Versus AI That Is The Automation [15:20] Reviewing Your Platform Before Renewal [17:09] Where To Find Jon Miller

Editor’s Note: This transcript has been edited for clarity and readability.

Heather Campbell: Thanks so much for joining us for today’s SEJ Podcast. I am your host, Heather Campbell, and I’ve got a guest who helped build the tools most of us have been running our marketing on for the last 20 years.

With me is Jon Miller. He is one of the people who invented the modern B2B marketing stack, and now he’s trying to replace it. I thought having him on our podcast would help marketing leaders take a look at whether the platforms you’re running could be causing gaps in the insights and data you need to grow your business.

Jon co-founded Marketo and was its CMO. He helped take it to a billion-dollar IPO, and the company went on to a $4.7 billion sale to Adobe. Then he did it again: He founded Engagio in 2015, an account-based marketing platform acquired by Demandbase in 2020, and ran their marketing through 2023.

Jon knows a thing or two about growth, and growth is where so many marketers and business leaders are stuck right now. That’s why I wanted to have you on the show. Jon, welcome.

Heather: As I learned about you and Phave.com, the newest platform you’ve just released, it’s barely even three weeks ago, right?

Heather: Tell us about what you saw breaking in traditional martech tools that made you want to compete with your own creation, thinking about Marketo. What gaps in current martech meant we marketers needed something new? What were you seeing?

Jon: First off, I love the name Phave for the company because it has such a positive association and it’s very short. The problem is I have to tell you how to spell it, which I think a search-focused audience might appreciate. It’s Phave with a PH: P-H-A-V-E.

With every other company, I’ve focused on wanting you to know how to spell it. Marketo and Engagio were pretty clear. I considered calling this company Conducto to put it into the same family, but ultimately decided Phave was better.

We launched Phave just over two and a half weeks ago after being in stealth for a while. For those who don’t know, Phave is AI-native marketing automation. It’s meant to replace legacy marketing automation platforms like Marketo, HubSpot, Eloqua, and Pardot.

As you alluded to, over 20 years ago, I was the founder of one of those platforms, Marketo. I’m proud of what we built. So many people come up to me who have literally built their careers on Marketo. That gives me such a positive feeling.

At the same time, when I decided to start a new company and was talking to people during my research, so many expressed frustration with the legacy platforms, using words like “necessary evil” to describe them.

I realized there was a lot of latent frustration with the lack of innovation in the category, especially since Marketo went to Adobe, Eloqua went to Oracle, and Pardot went to Salesforce.

People weren’t really moving on. There wasn’t much platform switching because there wasn’t anything worth switching to, and nobody likes a migration. So I set out to build it.

Jon: I didn’t want to build something a little better. Nobody is going to migrate for something a little better. There were two key principles behind Phave.

First, I wanted to build something for what I call the new B2B playbook. We built Marketo and the other systems for a fairly simplistic model of marketing: Maybe you have an ebook, get people to fill out a form, capture their information, nurture and score them, and pass them to sales at the right time.

It was very focused on new business acquisition and meant to be measurable within this simple, linear machine. That doesn’t reflect how buying works today. I’m not sure it ever really reflected how buying worked.

We know there are complex buying committees. Buying isn’t happening through one person.

Jon: We know a lot of that buying committee is doing research anonymously across channels that are hidden or dark to us. We know the initial land is usually just a small fraction of the total deal value, and we need to do post-sale marketing to retain and expand the relationship.

In many ways, tools like Marketo weren’t built for any of that. When I talk about the new playbook, I mean something that works the way marketing needs to work today.

Jon: That leads to the second principle: AI. AI has dramatically changed both how buyers buy and how we can market to them, and what’s possible.

With Phave, we set out not just to add AI to a marketing automation platform, but to reimagine what a marketing automation platform should be when you have access to modern reasoning AI models.

This lets us move from a world of if-then rules to AI that can think and reason. Rules have a place. They’re good at things that must be true, like “Don’t email someone in Germany if you don’t have double opt-in consent.”

Jon: Rules are bad at handling ambiguity and judgment. The reality is that B2B buying is full of ambiguity and judgment.

Think of all the things we do in a marketing platform: What should this person’s score be? Are they sales-ready? What segment should they belong in? Which rep should I route them to? All the way down to: What actions should I take with this person to move her and her buying group toward an objective I’m trying to achieve?

That requires handling ambiguity, judgment, and reasoning, which rules cannot do. That’s the big unlock. Phave in five minutes: new playbook, AI reasoning, not rules.

Heather: One thing I was drawn to was your statement that leads are too narrow and companies, or accounts, are too broad.

With all the personalization we can do, and knowing that personalization helps move people down the funnel, I like looking at them as buying groups. You have different levels and roles within an organization. That helps you take personalized messaging and give it to the person in that role, wherever they are in the pipeline and their stage right now.

Jon: There are a couple of aspects to where buying groups really matter. You’re referring to understanding each person’s role and persona in the buying group, which is absolutely important.

The other thing is recognizing which product or solution that buying group is about, because that also determines what they’ll find relevant. But the most important thing is: Where is that buying group in its unique journey with you?

The problem with traditional ABM, for example, is that an account buys a product from you, so you call that account a customer. If you market to everybody in the account as though they’re a customer, you’ll be doing the wrong thing for people in buying groups that are buying other products or are at a different stage of the journey.

That’s why buying groups are the right object for B2B, and why leads are too narrow, and accounts are too broad.

Heather: Organizations depend on the data their marketing technology gives them: the insights and dashboards they’ve leaned on for so many years.

For businesses still running on a legacy stack and relying on those, what gaps could it be creating that they’re not aware of? What should they look out for, and why might they want to switch to a different platform and get out of that legacy mindset?

Jon: I’ve seen marketers suffer from what I call “marketing automation Stockholm syndrome.” They’ve had to learn to live with the quirks and idiosyncrasies of these platforms. They take it for granted that this is how things are. When exposed to another way of doing things, their first reaction is, “But that’s not the way things are.”

Your question is: What should prompt you to pick your head up and say there should be something different here, even without talking about the massive transformations AI enables?

I talk to CMOs who are frustrated with how long it takes to go from idea to campaign. They have an idea, and demand gen creates a brief, which they can now do really fast with Claude. But then they hand it to somebody in marketing operations, who puts it into a ticket queue, prioritizes it, and eventually builds the campaign in Marketo two weeks later.

Then it goes back to the demand gen person, and you lose another two weeks on back-and-forth. That isn’t how things need to be, especially in the age of AI. It’s how we’ve learned to live with Marketo’s idiosyncrasies.

Another example is finding yourself buying more and more stuff and expanding your tech stack to fix gaps in your core platform: lead routing, matching leads to accounts, deduplication, and even more sophisticated things like buying groups, post-sale marketing, and bringing in social buying signals.

When you have a legacy platform, these are all things you solve by buying more stuff. Perhaps that’s not the right answer. When you’re looking at your bloated tech stack, ask: Shouldn’t there be a better, simpler way to do this? That’s another signal I’d look at.

Heather: Absolutely. “We’ve always done it that way” is built into too many workflows.

Heather: You’ve drawn a distinction between AI that builds your automation and AI that is the automation. Why should a business leader care which one they’re buying?

Jon: When you have AI that builds the automation, it’s still building a rules-based, if-this-then-that workflow. If you want to account for more factors, scenarios, and nuance, that means more conditions and branches. We’ve all seen how any attempt to do this well quickly ends up with spaghetti: complexity that’s brittle and hard to manage.

Having AI build it for you is a quick recipe for building something more complex, brittle, complicated, and harder to understand, rather than better and smoother. When AI builds it, you’re just paving the cow path in some ways, making the old way faster.

AI that is the automation is when you rethink it so you can do things you couldn’t do before.

As an analogy, before Claude Opus 4.8 came around, engineers were being asked to code. We talked about copilots, where the engineer was still coding, but AI was alongside, making suggestions: “You could do this faster.” Maybe they’d ask it to quickly clean up a subroutine. A year and a half ago, that was state of the art, and it was pretty cool.

Now, the best engineers don’t write code anymore. They go to Cursor, their harness, and say, “This is what I want,” and AI builds it for them. That’s different. AI is the coding, as opposed to helping with the coding.

In some ways, that’s what revolutionized engineering, and I think, if we let it, it will revolutionize marketing.

Heather: As we talk about AI coming in and some of the pivots marketers need to make, we need to look at what we’re tracking, how we’re tracking it, how we’re moving leads down the pipeline, and how we’re thinking about them.

What’s one thing listeners can take back to their desks and implement tomorrow? If they have a legacy system and feel they have gaps, where should they start looking or auditing to decide whether a different platform is right for them?

Jon: The first thing I’d advise anybody on a legacy platform, and this is self-serving, but I also think it’s good advice, is: Do not renew anything for more than a year. The world is moving too fast to lock yourself into a multi-year contract.

Imagine having an old flip phone and locking yourself into a two- or three-year contract a month before the iPhone came out. You’d feel pretty sad when everyone else was doing all this cool stuff, and you were still pressing buttons to send simple text messages.

The first thing I’d do is know when your renewal is up and try to get ahead of it. Depending on the size of your organization, it might take two months or six months to properly evaluate and implement another solution.

Get ahead of that. You may choose Phave or something besides Phave, but don’t let yourself get caught behind a contract and a renewal date. That’s where I’d start.

Heather: That’s great advice. Anytime you’re signing multi-year contracts, it’s good for pricing, but you don’t know what else is coming out that you might miss.

Jon, thank you for your time. I appreciate your insights and everything you’ve shared with our audience. Before I let you go, where can they find you?

Jon: It’s been a pleasure. Thank you for having me on and for the conversation. Professionally, we’re at Phave.com. You can check out a demo, read resources, or contact sales if appropriate.

I’m also on LinkedIn as Jon Miller, writing about all this and more. Come connect and follow me there.

Heather: I appreciate it, Jon. Thank you.

Chelsea news: Estevao Willian in Brazil future boost as Carlo Ancelotti addresses winger's situation with Xabi Alonso

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Darren Plant | 10 Nov 2026 13:00

Carlo Ancelotti has reassured Chelsea winger Estevao Willian that he is in no danger of losing his place in the Brazil squad.

The 19-year-old has endured a difficult start to life under Xabi Alonso at Stamford Bridge, largely being left on the periphery of the Spaniard's plans.

Despite a recent start against Manchester United and his half-time introduction versus Sunderland on Sunday, Estevao has played just 305 minutes of club football in 2026-27.

Alonso has been of the opinion that he does not want to risk Estevao picking up more muscle injuries at this stage of his career.

Theoretically, the former Palmeiras starlet missing out on opportunities could have impacted on his chances with his national team.

Brazilian outlet UOL addresses Ancelotti having to deal with a number of situations where players are lacking game time for their respective clubs.

Nevertheless, when speaking on Estevao's minutes, Ancelotti gave every indication that the attacker would remain a key player on the international stage.

He said: "What we expect from Estevao is for him to show the qualities he has. We’ll keep following him. Even if he doesn’t play a single minute at Chelsea, we’ll keep following him until we decide he’s no longer useful to the national team.”

"As long as we think he’s useful, we’ll keep following him and giving him opportunities to develop as a 19-year-old. We have different objectives to Chelsea."

Estevao has already racked up 143 senior appearances for Palmeiras, Chelsea and Brazil.

For a player who is still five months short of his 20th birthday and has spent time on the sidelines with muscle injuries, it is a lot of football.

While Estevao would naturally like to play every game, Alonso is attempting to look at the long-term picture, and it is difficult to argue against his approach.

With Estevao gaining 132 minutes across the last two Premier League matches, he is match-fit for Brazil's showdown with Japan on Saturday.

'Avatar' Officially Unveils a Very Different Version of Zuko in New Book

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A bold hairstyle experiment almost changed the Zuko we know and love

2026 has been a fantastic year to be a fan of the Avatar franchise — the elemental fantasy saga, not the blue alien sci-fi movies. The animated sequel series Avatar: Seven Havens has been phenomenally successful, with a second season already confirmed, and Paramount Studios even has an Avatar fighting game that actually feels great to play. However, arguably the biggest win for Avatar fans this year was Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender. Set 12 years after Avatar: The Last Airbender ended, the movie reunited the gang for a new adventure. And even though it didn't get a theatrical release as originally promised, it was still a win for Team Avatar.

Now, Abrams Books is releasing a new art book with that reveals fresh details about Avatar: Aang: The Last Airbender.

Featuring insights from co-creators Michael Dante DiMartino and Bryan Konietzko, director Lauren Montgomery, and the extended animation team, this art book offers a behind-the-scenes look at what it took to bring the next chapter to life.

Thanks to Abrams Books, Polygon has an exclusive preview of the art book. Take a look at these two eye-opening pages below.

While every member of Team Avatar had a serious glow-up by the time of the movie, we felt one member got the biggest: Fire Lord Zuko, Aang’s former nemesis and later close friend and firebending mentor.

As she followed Aang and his friends into their 20s, character designer Christie Tseng wanted to explore how varied the returning main cast could be.

“Considering how often his hairstyle changed in the show, [Zuko] certainly earned the biggest hair silhouette update out of the main cast!” Tseng shares in the art book. “We explored many different hairstyles for him, including shoulder-length to long, flowing manes, worn in styles ranging from low buns to high ponytails.”

Throughout the animated series, Zuko’s hairstyle often reflected his role in the story. In season 1, he wore a top knot, in keeping with typical Fire Nation style. In season 2, Zuko and his uncle Iroh cut their hair to symbolize leaving behind their status and going on the run, leaving Zuko looking rather scruffy. Zuko returns to the top knot in season 3 and wears it for the last time in the series finale, after becoming Fire Lord.

In Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender, Zuko’s still got that traditional top knot. However, animator Ki Hyun Ryu says the team considered many different versions of Zuko’s look, including one that would have fit right in at a concert hall as he sang in front of millions of fans.

“Early versions of Zuko leaned a bit too much toward a ‘pretty boy’ look, but I strongly felt he needed to appear the most mature among Team Avatar, as he is the ruler of the Fire Nation,” Ryu explains in the art book. “I focused on adding stronger angles to his face and shifting away from a K-pop-idol vibe toward a more grounded, masculine presence that reflects a true imperial leader.”

It’s true that Zuko is gorgeous, and a more K-pop-inspired look may have been appealing — just look at the popularity of KPop Demon Hunters. But one of the highlights of Avatar: The Last Airbender was seeing how wonderfully mature the characters had become over the years. It’s notable to see that maturity reflected in the team’s desire to tell a serious, meaningful story, right down to the small character details.

Fans can now see even more of how Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender came together, with Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender: Art of the Movie now available for purchase.

Scientists Found the Secret to Happiness—And You May Already Know What It Is

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The answer isn’t money, status, or luxury. It’s something you probably already have—and overlook.

Where does happiness actually come from? Both optimists and pessimists would probably agree that it can’t be found in diamonds, vacations with a price tag heavier than the luggage, or a car that’s a status symbol. There really is truth to the often repeated but terribly clichéd phrase, “the best things in life are free.” Happiness is also more complex than those platitudes would have you believe.

It turns out that having meaningful relationships is one of those intangible things that leads to happiness in the long term. Researchers Säde Stenlund of Harvard University, Kelsey O’ Connor of the National Institute of Statistics and Economic Studies in Luxembourg, and Lara Akin of Simon Fraser University in Canada investigated the source of happiness in hundreds of previous studies and their own life experience. More money (besides what’s needed to live comfortably) obviously didn’t cause an increase in happiness, but spending more time with those who matter most was one of the secrets that probably isn’t so secret. Other factors included work and activities that are meaningful to an individual and intentional habits such as listening to others, investing in relationships, and holding on to positive experiences.

“Across the discussion, having basic needs met, relationships, meaningful work, and living in ways that align with one’s values and circumstances emerged as central themes,” the researchers said in a study recently published in the Journal of Happiness Studies, which they think is why there’s a “need to address declining youth well-being and inequalities in well-being [and the] potential to have a greater impact on societies and individuals’ lives.”

Relationships are evidently the single greatest determinant of happiness. While they’re often underrated in the whirlwind of our busy lives, spending more time and forging deeper connections with loved ones can have lasting results. This echoes findings from the World Happiness Report that have ranked relationships high in importance to consistently happy people year after year. Humans are social creatures. Because people need constant social interaction for a fulfilling life, a 2023 survey by the World Economic Forum that relied on decades of research found that the happiest people, regardless of income, were those who maintained the strongest personal relationships. Another finding was that happier people tend to be healthier and live longer. Whether platonic or romantic, with genetic relatives or chosen family, relationships are integral to happiness.

While situations in which work is a drag have been endlessly memed and turned into relatable comedies like Office Space and The Office, the truth about work remains that meaning is everything. Many studies reviewed in this research found that non-financial aspects of a job are surprisingly high contributors to happiness. An increase of trust in management is so important that its value in happiness equates to a substantial raise, and workplace environments that focus on increasing satisfaction just as much as efficiency are likely to have happier employees overall. Employees in such environments want to be there and are usually more engaged by default. UPenn psychologist Martin Seligman, who was not involved in this study, insists that it also goes the other way—the happiness of employees boosts that of employers.

Happiness doesn’t necessarily entail a constant stream of positivity. Adverse circumstances at one point or another are almost inevitable, but the researchers insist that being happy is more of a journey than a permanent state of being. Even during a difficult period, someone who’s adaptable and continues to shape their life around their relationships, strengths, and values is more likely to see a flash of sunlight sooner. Support is a lifeline. After 2,400 years, Aristotle’s famous statement that “Man is a social animal” still rings true.

Elizabeth Rayne is a creature who writes. Her work has appeared in Popular Mechanics, Ars Technica, SYFY WIRE, Space.com, Live Science, Den of Geek, Forbidden Futures and Collective Tales. She lurks right outside New York City with her parrot, Lestat. When not writing, she can be found drawing, playing the piano or shapeshifting.

How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

Elon Musk’s Terafab targets 1 million wafers a month after 2032

Terafab’s procurement team reportedly has already been to Japan, asking some toolmakers to deliver within months.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk’s Terafab, a joint venture of Tesla and SpaceX, has informally told equipment makers it is seeking to produce 1 million wafers per month after 2032, according to Financial News. About 70% of this output, or 700,000 wafers, is planned to go to memory, with more than 300,000 going to logic chips. Terafab’s procurement team has already visited Japan to ask some local toolmakers for delivery within months. The memory allotment would rival Samsung’s estimated DRAM production capacity if it were all of that type.

Samsung’s DRAM capacity was estimated last year at 650,000–700,000 wafers a month, while SK hynix is reportedly at about 550,000, with about 1 million a goal by 2030. The comparison has to be taken in context, as Terafab’s 700,000 measures all of its expected memory output, which could be NAND or DRAM, while the Samsung and SK hynix figures measure only DRAM. As it is, the memory portion alone would be 3.5 times the reported 200,000 at SK hynix’s Wuxi fab in China.

Many in the industry were skeptical when Musk announced the plans in March, but orders are now leading more companies to see a real business opportunity. The timeline is aggressive, as it usually takes three years or more to get from planning to volume at a leading-edge fab, and Terafab is targeting volume production in the second half of 2028. Procurement officials from the company reportedly visited Japan earlier this month to meet local suppliers and to work on details on bringing in equipment, with some suppliers asked to deliver within a few months.

The Elec also reports that Terafab representatives visited booths of about 10 Korean materials, parts, and equipment firms at SEMICON West, with some NDAs signed.

Logic chips are one thing — Jiji Press reports Terafab is working with Intel to adopt its 2nm and 1.4nm processes — but memory is another, and it’s not clear what type of memory is on the docket. Technical help from a Taiwanese company is under consideration for memory, but no partner has been named, leaving 70% of the planned wafers without a disclosed process technology.

Memory production tends to split between DRAM and NAND flash, the latter for non-volatile storage. A Tesla job listing for a Terafab Memory Process Integration Engineer, reported in August, covers end-to-end DRAM process integration and DRAM node development, and supports emerging memories such as MRAM, RRAM, and 3D DRAM. Terafab hasn’t said how its DRAM would be divided among products like HBM and LPDDR, both useful for AI and both mentioned in its job postings, or whether it expects to make and stack the DRAM itself or package chips it brings in.

Building a cutting-edge logic fab on U.S. soil is its own challenge, and Tesla and SpaceX lack chip-manufacturing experience. Even Musk, on a SpaceX call last month, called Terafab’s dates “very aggressive” and said he put “big error bars” on the timeline. The first SRAM test chip is targeted for around May or June 2027 at the Austin research facility.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

How TSMC might fit in remains open. TSMC says any fab with its involvement has to be run by TSMC, while Musk said his own companies would build and operate Terafab, a scenario which could leave TSMC as a space tenant. At least the spending plans are known: $16.8 billion for the first phase in Grimes County, Texas, with total investment of up to $119 billion if later phases proceed. A pilot plant could be completed as early as July 2027.

Total solar eclipse 2027 vs 2028 — Which one should you choose, Egypt or Australia?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

From six minutes of darkness in Egypt or totality over the Australian Outback, here are the pros and cons of the next two total solar eclipses.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For those who want to experience a total solar eclipse, the next two years present the best kind of problem. On Aug. 2, 2027, the so-called "eclipse of the century" will sweep across southern Spain, North Africa and the Middle East, bringing more than six minutes of totality to parts of Egypt. Then, on July 22, 2028, another exceptionally long total solar eclipse will cross Australia, putting the remote Outback and Sydney Harbor in the path of the moon's dark central shadow before it reaches New Zealand's beautiful South Island.

Both eclipses will be spectacular, and both will require careful planning, yet they promise very different experiences. The 2027 event combines over six minutes of totality with some of the very best weather prospects possible, while 2028 offers over five minutes of totality above some of the world's most famous wildernesses and cityscapes.

Eclipse chasing requires careful planning and dedication, but also significant funds. If you can only choose one, which should it be?

On paper, 2027 is difficult to beat. Maximum totality on Aug. 2 will last about 6 minutes, 23 seconds, making it the longest totality on land since 1991 and until 2114. The path of totality will cross southern Spain and Gibraltar before sweeping across Morocco, Algeria, Tunisia, Libya, Egypt, Sudan, Saudi Arabia, Yemen, Somalia and the Chagos Islands. About 88.9 million people live in its path, according to Time and Date. It will dominate the news for weeks.

For Europeans, southern Spain is the obvious easy option. Tarifa, at the country's southern tip, will see about 4 minutes, 39 seconds of totality, while the U.K. Overseas Territory of Gibraltar gets about 4 minutes, 25 seconds. Travel farther east and the duration increases, with Sfax in Tunisia getting around 5 minutes, 41 seconds and locations in Egypt experiencing more than six minutes. Weather prospects generally improve, too, as the path crosses the deserts of North Africa.

Egypt is the headline destination. Luxor will see about 6 minutes, 20 seconds of totality near midday, with Karnak Temple, the Valley of the Kings, and some of the world's greatest ancient monuments nearby. The climatic odds are excellent, with clouds extremely rare in early August. That combination of a very long totality, ancient landscapes and a very high probability of a clear sky makes 2027's path of totality highly unusual.

But there are downsides. Temperatures around Luxor can exceed 105°F (41°C) in August, a dust storm is not impossible, and totality occurs shortly after 1 p.m. when the sun (and moon) will be high overhead — making photography very difficult. Demand is already intense, making organized trips expensive. Spain will be considerably easier and probably cheaper for European eclipse chasers, but at the expense of roughly two minutes of totality — and slightly less favorable weather prospects.

If 2027 is about extreme duration, 2028 is about choice. On July 22, 2028, a path of totality will cross the Cocos Islands, Christmas Island, Australia, from northwest to southeast — passing directly over Sydney — before continuing across the Tasman Sea to New Zealand's South Island. That's about 6.3 million people, according to Time and Date. This will be an exclusive eclipse experienced by relatively few people, certainly when compared to the eclipses in 2024, 2026 and 2027.

Maximum totality will last 5 minutes and 10 seconds in the remote Kimberley region of Western Australia. That's more than a minute shorter than 2027, but still exceptionally long by total solar eclipse standards (most last about two minutes). More importantly, the Australian Outback has excellent climatic prospects. Historical cloudiness on July 22 is around 8% at Purnululu (Bungle Bungles) close to the point of greatest eclipse in Western Australia and 19% at Karlu Karlu (Devil's Marbles) in the Northern Territory, making Australia's interior an attractive destination for serious eclipse chasers. Temperatures will be around 86°F (30°C) in July.

Then there's Sydney. Australia's largest city will experience about 3 minutes, 45 seconds of totality, its first total solar eclipse since 1857. Sydney Opera House and Harbor Bridge beneath a totally eclipsed sun could produce some of the decade's defining astronomical images, while millions of people experiencing totality together should create an extraordinary atmosphere. Temperatures will be around 62.6°F (17°C).

A compromise is weather. Historical cloudiness in Sydney is around 43%, which is good odds for most eclipses — and July is known for clear weather. But in 2028, anyone prioritizing their chances of a clear totality will be tempted by a (albeit pricier) trip to the Outback. For Europeans, reaching Australia also comes with considerable cost and time. The huge number of hotels in Sydney could keep accommodation relatively competitive, but the long flight means it makes little sense to travel there for only a few days. For many international eclipse chasers, 2028 will naturally become an expensive one- or two-week trip.

There's another reason to think beyond those few minutes of darkness. With the right planning, an eclipse trip can also become an extraordinary astronomy vacation (tip: come a week before the eclipse for the darkest evening skies). Here, 2028 offers visitors from Europe and North America something very different — the Southern Hemisphere night sky.

July is a superb time to see the bright central regions of the Milky Way from dark locations in Australia. The Southern Cross, or Crux, is one of the southern sky's defining sights, with Alpha and Beta Centauri nearby. Omega Centauri, the largest and brightest globular cluster visible from Earth, is another showpiece, while the Eta Carinae region offers one of the richest sections of the Milky Way for binoculars and small telescopes.

There are other reasons to extend the trip. A partial lunar eclipse occurs on July 6-7, 2028, while the Southern Delta Aquariid meteor shower peaks around July 28 — a week after the eclipse, during a first-quarter moon — and is particularly well seen from the Southern Hemisphere. Add Australia's world-class dark skies, and a trip into the Outback could become as much about stargazing as the eclipse itself.

The 2027 eclipse has fewer night-sky extras. The Delta Aquariid meteor shower peaks a few days before the eclipse — in dark, moonless skies — while Jupiter will dominate the western sky shortly after sunset. However, anyone traveling from the Northern Hemisphere will largely see a familiar celestial hemisphere. Australia offers a genuinely different sky experience.

Perhaps the most useful way to choose between these eclipses is not to compare their durations, but to imagine where you would most like to be when the moon's shadow arrives.

In 2027, the decision largely comes down to how far along the eclipse track you want to travel. Stay in southern Spain for convenience and around four minutes of totality, head into Tunisia for longer totality and excellent weather prospects, or go all-in on Egypt for more than six minutes beneath almost cloudless desert skies — and bragging rights among eclipse chasers. It's an eclipse that can be combined with beaches, Roman ruins, North African cities or the temples and, of course, the tombs of ancient Egypt.

In 2028, the contrast is even greater. Sydney offers a communal total solar eclipse — a busy "we eclipse" — with millions watching daylight disappear above one of the world's great cities. Head thousands of miles away into the Australian interior and the same event becomes a lonely "me eclipse" in the wilderness, with up to five minutes of totality above an immense Outback landscape. Continue to New Zealand, and there's yet another possibility — a shorter totality with the sun much lower in the afternoon sky.

If your priority is purely totality duration, 2027 marginally wins. More than six minutes of totality combined with extraordinarily reliable weather is a rare combination. There's a good reason Aug. 2, 2027 is being marketed as the "eclipse of the century." But if you've always wanted to visit Australia or New Zealand, the 2028 eclipse is an extraordinary excuse to finally go, in addition to experiencing a long totality.

Choose the trip you most want to take, then find the best possible place for you within the path of totality. After all, no total solar eclipse lasts long enough, and most eclipse chasers struggle to recall exactly how long totality lasted. They're just left with the same question as always — when is the next one?

Jamie is an experienced science and travel journalist, stargazer and eclipse chaser who writes about exploring the night sky, solar and lunar eclipses, the Northern Lights, moon-gazing, astro-travel, astronomy and space exploration. He is the editor of WhenIsTheNextEclipse.com, author of A Stargazing Program For Beginners, co-author of The Eclipse Effect, and a senior contributor at Forbes.

Ransomware threat blares from Vanderbilt University computers during cyberattack

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

BYOD claims extensive data theft as campus Wi-Fi returns, most systems remain restricted, and classes carry on

Classes at Vanderbilt University are proceeding as scheduled today, although access to most university systems remains restricted following a cyberattack claimed by ransomware group BYOD.

Vanderbilt announced on Monday morning that it was dealing with "an active cybersecurity incident," and that the fallout may affect university devices, systems, and cloud-based services.

The Nashville-based university initially told students and staff to shut down university devices and disconnect from campus Wi-Fi. It warned them to watch for unexpected prompts, altered files or permissions, unfamiliar applications, and unsolicited authentication requests.

Campus computers reportedly began playing an audio threat attributed to BYOD. Student newspaper The Vanderbilt Hustler cited posts on campus social network Fizz showing the message.

"You have been compromised by BYOD," the recording said. "We suggest you reach out to us as soon as possible. Instructions have been placed on your desktop. Failure to communicate will result in this estate being brought to the ground completely. Make the right choice or rot with the wrong one."

By Monday evening, Vanderbilt had restored access to campus Wi-Fi, but warned that access to most systems remains restricted.

"We know this has disrupted important work and caused frustration across the university," it said. "Vanderbilt Information Technology is working with government agencies and other partners to manage this situation."

Students were told to attend classes as scheduled on Tuesday and contact instructors about flexibility on coursework deadlines. Staff were instructed to report to work normally.

Vanderbilt's Athletics department said that its ticketing system was unavailable, and that students were unable to claim tickets for the college's football game against Alabama on Saturday.

BYOD claimed responsibility for the attack on Vanderbilt on Monday, along with extensive data theft.

"If you could not tell already, your databases are locked [and] we have your entire estate and research data," it said via its leak site.

"Over 1 million SSNs, full names, email addresses, phone numbers, student and employee records, full takeover of your DC, whatever was on your network, we possess."

Vanderbilt has not confirmed BYOD's claims about the extent of its access or the data allegedly stolen.

BYOD may be a familiar name to some. The group previously claimed attacks on the University of New Mexico and Trump Mobile's customer database.

BYOD also claimed responsibility on Monday for an attack on Texas Health and Human Services. The agency has not publicly confirmed an incident and did not immediately respond to our questions. ®

How Xtube Kill the Porn Studio

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

For decades, anyone driving down the far-east stretch of one of Toronto’s main arteries would likely have caught a glimpse of a white Cadillac perched atop the entrance of a strip mall beckoning them to stop and step through a set of dark blue doors, behind which lay a world of male fantasy. So iconic was Caddy’s that when the strip club closed in 2017 the Toronto Sun sent out a reporter to mark the moment. In a story titled ‘Last Dance’, the paper somewhat melodramatically bewailed the end of an institution that had provided thousands upon thousands of Torontian men with an ‘escape from the drudgery of [ . . .] lives, wives and troubles’.

Judging by the long streak of one-star ratings on Caddy’s now inactive reviews page, its glory days were long gone by the time the classic car was taken down for good—and it wasn’t the only strip club to have suffered such a fate. By 2017 only twelve clubs were still operating out of the nearly fifty joints that had been dotted around Toronto at the turn of the century. Strip clubs have always been controversial, criticized for monetizing the objectification of women in a way many find too crude. Yet the stripper—at once desired and despised, exploited yet feared for her power to corrupt the good man—has not vanished. She permeates popular culture, from blockbusters and music videos to fitness crazes. Why, then, have strip clubs been shutting down? The reason was clear to Canadian stripper and activist Valerie Scott. “It’s called the internet,” she said in the report on Caddy’s closure; now, anyone seeking to escape the drudgery of reality had only to turn to their phone.

As fate would have it, Caddy’s made a cameo appearance in the story of how adult entertainment moved online. A little over a decade before the club played its last song it hired a man named Kurtis Potec, a former welder who’d just traded his tools for the DJ booth. “It wasn’t great; it wasn’t horrible,” Kurtis told me over a video call years later. “It was something to pay the bills.” Young and restless, he was a jack of all trades trying to find his way in life, but playing the tunes to which strippers danced and derobed turned out not to be Kurtis’s ultimate calling either. When he spotted a newspaper ad for a desk job at an online start-up not far from Caddy’s he figured he’d try his luck in the emerging virtual world.

“That was probably one of the most unique interviews of my life,” Kurtis told me with a chuckle. After a cursory skim of the twenty-something’s CV, the interviewer began to spout some “pretty vulgar, sexual-related terms” to check whether Kurtis was prepared for the world of online pornography. On the internet, there were no gatekeepers to control what people said, “so I guess they wanted to prepare me for the worst.”

He got the job. Kurtis’s new employer was Xtube, the first tube site targeted at gay men. The start-up’s first office was housed in a warehouse on the outskirts of the city, piled up to the ceiling and wall to wall with boxes, leaving only a two-foot-wide passage for Kurtis and his handful of co-workers to reach their desks. Inside the perilously stacked towers were countless pornographic DVDs awaiting digitization and to be uploaded online, along with merchandise like mouse pads and socks emblazoned with the company logo. Other than the three founders and the main financier—a Chinese man whom the others referred to as Mr X—the only other employee was a video encoder who at times appeared to live in the back room, where he worked and chain-smoked in solitude.

Kurtis, by contrast, was drawn into a far more public role. When one of the co-founders left, spooked by the threats that started rolling in from porn producers angry at finding their films on Xtube, Kurtis essentially picked up the day-to-day running of the site. Soon, he became the face of the company. Literally. “If you had any issues on the site, there was my ugly mug plastered in the corner,” he said. His associates didn’t use their real names, let alone their faces, and they had Kurtis—still technically a junior employee—front the company. “They were older and smarter than I was,” he told me, laughing.

By 2008, a year after Pornotube halted user uploads over fears of illegal content, business at Xtube was booming. Each month, it was pulling in hundreds of thousands of dollars from advertising. Advertisers were enthralled by this new medium, which allowed them to target their messages with unprecedented precision. Traditional media, including TV and magazines, had long promised access to certain demographics, but the internet offered the ability to drill deeper and glean details about people’s lives without asking a single question. Those being tracked hardly resisted. Many did not grasp the scale of the surveillance, while others were simply distracted by all the free stuff, like porn. Xtube’s users, Kurtis said, “really liked it.” But most of his days were spent fielding calls from within the industry, and their reaction was less enthusiastic. “People hated us.”

Around this time Kurtis started feeling depressed and began using drugs to cope with a job that, more and more, involved people shouting at him. “I got very used to hearing the words: I’m going to sue you.” But the threats were toothless. Free-speech laws governing the internet meant that user-generated platforms were, in essence, not legally responsible for what users uploaded.

All the studios could do was stand by and watch as their films were repeatedly stolen and re-uploaded by fans and, insiders complained, by tube sites themselves. It didn’t take long before their powerlessness began to attract investors’ attention. User-generated platforms seemed legally untouchable. And while there were plenty of platforms selling ad space, few received as many visits as the free porn sites. Kurtis was told to fend off callers claiming they wanted to buy Xtube. But one day in 2008 a man on the line refused to take no for an answer. He kept repeating: “Just give me a number.”

Kurtis walked into the back room, where one of the owners was sitting, and asked if there was any amount of money that would make it worth parting with Xtube. The slightly facetious reply was 35 million Canadian dollars. ‘I walked back, picked up the phone and said: “You’re not gonna like this,”’ Kurtis recalled. The man on the phone didn’t hesitate; he’d be there early next week to sort out the details.

In the months that followed a German in his early thirties would regularly occupy the carpet by Kurtis’s desk. Fabian Thylmann would stretch out on it to ease a back battered by his now frequent flights to Toronto. Fabian was a self-proclaimed nerd who as a teenager in the late nineties had developed one of the earliest pieces of software capable of tracking what internet users clicked on. Crucially, it allowed website owners to earn money when a link on their page redirected a visitor to make a purchase elsewhere. Hawking online advertising space had been a fairly unsurprising evolution of traditional marketing, but here was something that made it possible to monitor what people did next, and profit from it. The internet was turning into a game of digital chess, with money to be made by those who could anticipate, and manipulate, the whims of anyone who entered their domain.

Fabian’s first company had given him a rare glimpse into the back end of some early online businesses. Many were trying to sell porn, which was one of the first things people were willing to pay for on the internet. But these were small, makeshift operations, and few were making real money. In the early noughties, most of the people buying smutty DVDs or renting pay-per-views alone in hotel rooms hardly knew how to use the internet. Sure, they would eventually find their way to adult sites. But Fabian realized that a truly successful enterprise could do far more than tap into existing demand—it could bring pornography to people who had not even looked for it, through pop-ups, ads and search results, turning it from something you searched for into something that found you. Once porn became part of the internet’s background noise, those who’d never have dared to cross the threshold of a sex shop or an erotic cinema, or breach any of the other hurdles the curious had once faced, could now stumble upon porn with a single click.

The purpose of tube sites, like that of virtually every internet platform since, was not to replicate what existed in the physical world but to construct a new, disembodied mode of interaction built around visual stimuli—what we now call “content.” Their rise marked the start of a new era, when every step online began to be tracked. It was a turning point in the story of how data, harvested without people’s informed consent, became one of the world’s most valuable commodities, eventually proliferating to a point where it could be used to teach artificial intelligence to think and act like us. The tale of tube sites, then, is also the story of how the digital world came to be organized around priorities that do not necessarily align with those of the people who inhabit it.

The practice Fabian helped pioneer as a teenager is today known as affiliate marketing—put simply, the business of getting people to click on things. It’s how content creators make money when they offer their followers discounts on whatever must-have food supplement, skin cream or therapy app they claim to love. And the business of funneling people’s attention was first stress-tested in the porn industry. Long before influencers started peddling protein powder, porn sites were paying webmasters to drive clicks to their content, offering a cut for every paying visitor who came to their site through a tracked banner ad or link. It was the logic of the early thumbnail galleries, like the one built by the pale programmer who had filched sandwiches from Mario Salieri.

One of the key reasons why Amazon grew to become the place for online shopping was its early adoption of an affiliate programme which paid website owners a commission to direct people to products sold on the platform. Once people got accustomed to buying all their stuff on Amazon, the companies that actually made the stuff—anything from phone chargers to vacuum cleaners to cat litter—realized that if their products weren’t on the site, no one would buy them. Needless to say, affiliate marketing is still one of the main legs of Amazon’s business model, and its foundations were laid by people like Fabian, who understood that to make real money online you need to attract plenty of people and make sure they keep coming back. This is called “the network effect,” and it is one of the basic laws of the digital economy—the more people use your service, the more powerful you become.

As sites such as Xtube were attracting a critical mass of visitors, they could essentially dictate terms to studios that were now, in desperation, drawn into partnerships with the platforms. The tube site’s offer was to feature ten or fifteen minutes of footage and call it “advertising.” The only problem was that the average visit to a porn site ends—rather abruptly—after about nine minutes.

Still, as free content washed over the Web, studios started uploading their own clips to tube sites, hoping a few hundred or thousand viewers might click the affiliate link and pay for the full film. If they did, the revenue would be split 50:50 with the tube site. “We put a lot of studios out of business,” Kurtis said, “they couldn’t afford to get traffic from us.” Companies like Xtube quickly learned to steer what users clicked on next, and once they controlled enough traffic to funnel millions of potential customers towards paying sites or advertisers, they became powerful.

This dynamic has since spread beyond porn. Many news outlets now depend on posting their stories to Facebook, TikTok or X, hoping to lure readers back to their own sites, where some might eventually pay for their journalism. Few do. Like porn, news has become something most of us expect to get for free. The number of independent newspapers has collapsed, many swallowed by conglomerates that rely on scale to profit from advertising and, increasingly, from affiliate links.

Fabian’s ambitions were clear from the beginning. Xtube was offering more than a quick buck. It was a stepping stone in the making of an empire. He wanted to “basically take over the adult industry,” Kurtis said. As the traditional studios buckled under the tube-site business model, many went up for sale, which meant that their huge libraries of content could be bought on the cheap. For a site like Xtube, buying studios and their subscription platforms made perfect sense. It gained access to more content, and could funnel its huge traffic towards paid sites it now controlled. If people paid for porn, the company profited; if they preferred the free stuff, it profited too.

It was a business model that thrived on relentless expansion, and Fabian had his sights set on a much bigger rival. In nearby Montreal, another group of young men were running a site that would become to pornography what McDonald’s is to the burger: Pornhub. To simplify, it was founded in 2007 by three Concordia University students—Matt Keezer, Steph Manos and Sam Youssef—whose company, Mansef, was already running several porn businesses, including the still influential production studio Brazzers (a slang take on “brothers”). Publicly, Youssef posed as a concerned studio owner and railed against tube sites on the industry forum GoFuckYourself.com: “They will not steal it and get away with it. Their days are counted!” What few in the industry realized was that Pornhub was run by the same group.

In a 2023 interview about his most recent venture Youssef said that he and his co-founders made the decision to sell Pornhub to Fabian in 2010 for roughly $140 million because he “didn’t feel comfortable at all with what the company had become.” He sounded almost surprised to have found himself in the sex industry, despite having made his money from sites such as JuggFuckers and RacksAndBlacks.

When I tried to contact them all in 2020, Youssef declined to talk to me about those early years, saying he was never really “much involved” with the business he had founded. Keezer likewise responded: “To be honest with you, I never had any involvement in the ‘industry.’ I just made Pornhub in 2007 with a couple of friends and sold it [ . . .] and that was about it.” I had no response from Manos. This detachment was typical of the internet entrepreneurs who profited from unleashing pornography. Many preferred to remain anonymous, even as the industry’s most important workers literally have to bare all. In a move that reflected the trolling culture of the nerds who were taking over porn, Fabian—the new king of smut—renamed his empire Manwin.

Denis Villeneuve Is GQ’s Director of the Year

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Where some other awards-bestowing committees have been notoriously late in giving great directors their flowers, GQ refuses to waste time. Which is why we’ve honored Denis Villeneuve as our Director of the Year for 2026—yes, for Dune: Part Three, due out December 25th, but also for turning a Hollywood-stymying sci-fi classic into a three-part visual feast that we wish didn’t have to end. So before it does, GQ will be celebrating Villeneuve tonight, in Los Angeles.

The French-Canadian is, of course, more than just the director of Dune. He’s an Academy Award winner who can turn tentpole spectacles into critical darlings. As GQ’s Zach Baron writes in his profile of Villeneuve, out next week, “His signature films—2015’s cartel thriller Sicario, or 2016’s science fiction film Arrival, which was nominated for eight Academy Awards—are knotty and severe and not always easy to follow; they are also beautiful and seem to play well anywhere, whether on an airplane, in an HBO rerun, or at an IMAX theater.”

He is also, now, about to embark on another massive adventure, though this time in our solar system: Villeneuve will be directing the next James Bond film.

To celebrate the man behind the camera, GQ will host a conversation between Villeneuve and Zach Baron, host of One More Question, followed by a special screening of Dune: Part Two to get everyone ready for the third-and-final installment. This all happens tonight at the newly opened, architecturally stunning Lucas Museum of Narrative Art in Los Angeles.

Come back next Tuesday to read Baron’s profile of Denis Villeneuve, part of GQ’s Men of the Year issue.
