Andrew Scott On the Real-Life Story Behind the His Heartbreaking Performance in the film, ‘Elsinore’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In the fourth scene of the fourth act of Hamlet, our melancholy hero stands on a desolate Danish plain watching a Norwegian army march into Poland on a trivial errand. Turning to the audience, in the last of his soliloquies, he imagines “the imminent death of twenty thousand men that for a fantasy and trick of fame go to their graves like beds.”

How must it have felt for Ian Charleson, the sandy-​haired Scottish actor who rose to international stardom in Chariots of Fire but was dying of AIDS by the time he played his own legendary Hamlet at London’s National Theatre in 1989, to utter those lines? A silent, unjust war raged around him, as young men all over London were dying in isolation, their sheets burned as soon as their bodies were carted out, or, if they were lucky, driven home to take their final breaths in their childhood beds, eliciting whispers from the neighbors.

There is no way to know whether Hamlet, a role steeped in questions about life and death, was ever before played by a man who understood that his own death was so close. Those extraordinary circumstances are what animate Elsinore, the new film directed by Simon Stone that revisits and rejoices in Charleson’s final months. Many great actors have described their art as a means of escape—from the humdrum of reality, from the cage of the self. Andrew Scott, the Irish actor who is playing Charleson in Elsinore to a degree of fanfare approaching Oscar preordainment, does not share this view. Nor, he suspects, did Ian Charleson.

“The more I act,” Scott tells me from the quietest recess of a West Hollywood hotel restaurant on a 100-degree October day, “the more I see it not as pretending to be someone else but as exploring different parts of myself. Who would I be if I was born in this era or in this body or in this set of circumstances? A great actor once said that acting is an escape from acting. I think there’s a lot of sense in that.”

If I’ve understood Scott correctly, whereas living can at times be a kind of pantomime, the stage—or the screen—offers the promise of truth. Hamlet tells his own troupe of players that acting ought to hold the mirror up to nature. It’s hard not to think of that famous line early in Elsinore, when Charleson, in the privacy of his Hampstead flat, scans the contours of his body with a blue plastic hand mirror, desperately hoping for another day free from the blue-brown lesions of Kaposi’s sarcoma that for many in the early days of HIV announced the progression to full-blown AIDS.

Scott, who turned 50 last month, grew up in Dublin in the shadow of the AIDS crisis and, like so many gay men of his age, has often thought how lucky he is to have been born just a few years later than the generation that died en masse. With better education and better treatment, men of his era survived, but for them sex and death could never be wholly disentangled.

“The fact that the current generation of young gay people feels a freedom from that is wonderful,” Scott says. He began his professional stage career at the Abbey Theatre in Dublin, in 1996, and he remembers what he calls “a conspiracy of silence around sexuality” at the time, a carryover from the beginning of the crisis when governments refused to speak or act. Elsinore revives not only the National Theatre of the 1980s but also the gay bars and cruising grounds of Hampstead Heath, where the Sisters of Perpetual Indulgence, a group of activists who dressed as Catholic nuns, educated gay men on safe practices at a time of governmental neglect. “People would ask them, ‘What do we do? How do we protect ourselves?’ ” says Scott, who learned more about Charleson and his era from his friend Stephen Beresford’s rigorously researched script. “Because there was no information, no help, all this silence. The terror of that silence is the thing that offends. That should be where the shame lies.”

Though Scott found mainstream success on British TV shows such as Sherlock and Phoebe Waller-Bridge’s Fleabag—as the so-called Hot Priest—he has remained dedicated to the theater and just completed a two-week run of Simon Stephens’s 50-minute monologue Sea Wall in New York. (In fact the play’s 19 performances were filmed as part of an upcoming motion picture about the process of creating theater.) In recent years Scott has taken on a pair of haunted gay roles, in Netflix’s stylish retelling of the Ripley story and, opposite Paul Mescal, in 2023’s All of Us Strangers. And yet he is still the rare openly gay actor whose sexuality has been greeted with a collective shrug.

“Well, it isn’t very interesting,” he says, shaking his head. “What was so insidious was that Ian and his predecessors had something to contribute to the film and television industry but weren’t given the opportunities in the same way, or else they were given the opportunities but they were also given a sort of silent choice: You can have that, but you have to conceal a huge part of your identity in return, while their straight counterparts didn’t have to make that sacrifice. In fact that part of their identity was celebrated rather than concealed or derided. I do feel that the playing field is starting to level out. There’s that great expression that prejudice doesn’t survive proximity. If you see people, you become less frightened of them.”

FACE FORWARD “He has no shield,” says costar Olivia Colman of Scott. “An instant and direct route to any emotion. He is the most beautiful empath.” Dior jacket and collar.

In the film Charleson bemoans the fact that all anyone remembers of Chariots of Fire, which won best picture at 1982’s Academy Awards, is the theme music. For those who came up in London’s theater scene when he did, the telling of his story feels overdue. Charleson was, by all accounts, a beloved figure in his artistic circle: clever and churlish, an excellent dancer and singer, abundantly generous to young actors. Hilton McRae, a Scottish actor who first met Charleson as a fellow student at the University of Edinburgh and was a close friend throughout his life, has seen Elsinore three times. “Andrew is Ian Charleson,” he says. “There’s that same sense of mischief. That same wicked wit. And I think off-screen he’s got the same kind of Celtic integrity about how to run his life.”

McRae missed his old friend’s Hamlet because he was busy playing Macbeth in his hometown of Dundee. So for him the film has been an almost uncanny invitation to enter into Charleson’s mind at a crucial moment. “When he had to say the lines ‘we defy augury. There’s a special providence in the fall of a sparrow. If it be now, ’tis not to come; if it be not to come, it will be now; if it be not now, yet it will come. The readiness is all.’ How do you say these things knowing what’s coming? How much was he hoping, how much was he in a kind of denial? I saw him when he’d finished. I went round to look after him one day, and he had Much Ado About Nothing and King Lear by his bedside. He was looking at Benedick, and he was looking at Lear. He wanted to do them. The marvelous thing about this film is that it’s basically about how important art is.”

For Juliet Stevenson, who plays Gertrude, Hamlet’s mother, in Elsinore, making the film offered an almost mystic mix of art and memory. Stevenson starred in Hedda Gabler at the National Theatre at the very moment when Charleson played his Hamlet; the two plays were in repertory on the National’s main stage, the Olivier. Years later, in 2017, she played Gertrude when Andrew Scott took on the role of Hamlet in an acclaimed run in London’s West End. She first met Ian Charleson when she was 20 and newly out of the Royal Academy of Dramatic Art. An actor had broken her leg in a production of The Tempest at the Royal Shakespeare Company in Stratford-upon-Avon, and Stevenson joined the cast while they were in dress rehearsals. Charleson was Ariel, and she remembers seeing him suspended over the stage by wires singing “Full fathom five.”

Scott as Charleson in a still from Elsinore.

“He was very beautiful, very camp, very kind of golden,” she recalls. “It’s easy to sentimentalize people who die young, who have a story like Ian’s, but he really was adored. He was supremely witty, funny, naughty, and acerbic, but kind, generous. Men or women—we were all a little bit in love with him.” Stevenson will never forget the moment she greeted Charleson at the National in 1989, not having seen him since Stratford. “Backstage the National is a great big community. Every show shares the green room, the canteen, the corridors. I was in a crowded elevator, waiting before the doors shut, and I looked across this landing and saw Ian walking towards the bar. I said, ‘Hey, Ian! Hi!’ He turned and said, ‘Hi, Juliet!’ I said, ‘Welcome to the National. How are you doing?’ He said, ‘Well, they say I’m dying! Isn’t that crazy?’ I just kind of stared at him. And then the elevator doors shut. The young man who’d been trying to keep it secret saw me and launched that message across that landing with a kind of strange gaiety. But—he was playing Hamlet! He turned his suffering into art. I hope that doesn’t sound pretentious, but I do believe that’s what he did.”

The director Richard Eyre’s much-ballyhooed staging of Hamlet arrived at the National with Daniel Day-Lewis in the title role; the actor was making a sort of homecoming to the stage as a newly minted movie star. (He would go on to win an Oscar for his performance in My Left Foot the next year.) Day-Lewis’s Hamlet was perhaps doomed by unreasonably high expectations; he did not receive the expected raves, and in September 1989, in the middle of a scene in which the prince confronts his father’s ghost, he walked off the stage and out of the production. Day-Lewis has not performed onstage since that night.

If there’s something Eyre disputes in Elsinore, where he is played by the actor Johnny Flynn, it is the suggestion that he offered Charleson the opportunity to take over Hamlet with a kind of benevolent calm. Charleson had been a huge success as Sky Masterson in Eyre’s production of Guys and Dolls at the National in 1982, and Eyre regarded him as a hugely capable classical actor too. But casting him this time was fraught with danger. “It was unimaginably painful,” Eyre remembers. “Daniel was a very close friend, and it was a terrible shock when he dropped out. I made the decision to cast Ian with the knowledge that he was ill and in defiance of a lot of my colleagues, who thought I was making a big mistake. My character appears absolutely stoical and generous, but in reality I was a total mess.”

The film acknowledges Charleson’s reluctance to come out publicly and even, at first, to his Hamlet castmates. Ian McKellen had come out a year earlier, on a live BBC broadcast, in part to protest Section 28, a Thatcher-era law that banned schools from “intentionally promoting homosexuality.” Charleson, always out to his friends, felt that the circumstances of his career made public disclosure impossible. “McKellen went on to become this astonishing global movie star, but at the time he didn’t have a movie career to worry about,” Eyre explains. “Ian Charleson had starred in Chariots and had a significant presence in Gandhi. He was lined up to be the next Robert Redford. I think he would have matured into a real international film star. But interestingly, he wanted more to do theater. He thought being a movie star was slightly ridiculous, as of course it is.”

At a moment when many queer people in the United States feel less safe, and when American investment in combating HIV globally has evaporated with the dismantling of USAID, Elsinore has a sense of political urgency that its creators may not have envisioned for it. While Scott knows that the film does not depart wholly from the somber tenor of his recent screen roles, he plays Charleson for laughs because humor was his essential mode, and he regards his unlikely Hamlet—with its four thousand lines over four hours—as a herculean effort against all odds.

“In some ways my fear was that we would fetishize Ian’s death rather than celebrate his life,” Scott says. “And actually his decision to play Hamlet at the very end of his life, to nail his colors to the mast in that way, when time was running out, is so life-loving.” Margaret Johnson, the doctor who cared for Charleson in his final months and who spoke extensively with Stephen Beresford as he was researching the screenplay, remembered occasions when he would come to her office the morning after a performance looking especially beleaguered. “Not tonight,” she would tell him. He would acquiesce, but then Johnson would watch him out her office window hailing a cab to the National. “As he says, I’d rather die of Hamlet than of AIDS,” Scott adds, quoting his character. “That’s somebody who wants to live. That’s important for me to say.”

Olivia Colman, who plays Johnson in Elsinore, was drawn to the tension between her character’s responsibility for the preservation of life and Charleson’s wish to focus on joy, even if it cost him time. “Art and joy make life worth living. He really proved that,” she says. Colman, like Eyre, thinks Charleson would be proud of Scott’s portrayal. “Oh, Andrew! He has no shield. An instant and direct route to any emotion. He is the most beautiful empath, and because it’s real, he’s not acting. He’s being.”

Scott and his two siblings lost their mother in 2024, and he says that work has had a grounding effect in the aftermath. Hollywood actors often speak of the necessity to strike while the iron is hot, and if he has the glorious awards season that has been augured for him (Scott would be the first openly gay man to win an acting Oscar), he will have his pick of roles in 2027. But instead he would like to do less. “I think it’s time to get my work-life balance back a bit, to sort of smell the roses,” he says. He wouldn’t mind a romantic comedy, or even a musical. If Charleson had King Lear at his bedside, perhaps it was because he knew that dying young would prevent him from doing the thing great Shakespearean actors, such as John Gielgud and Laurence Olivier, do: play Hamlet in their relative youth and play Lear as they age. But for Scott, such ambitions are far from the mind.

In this story: hair, Moe Mukai; grooming, Victoria Bond; tailor, Mirpuri Bespoke.

The Gun Industry Is Divided Over Federal’s New High-Pressure 6.5 Creedmoor Ammo. Here’s Why

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 10, 2026 1:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

Late last month Bergara became the first gun manufacturer to explicitly state Federal’s 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak ammo is not approved for use in its bolt-action rifles. This reverses earlier guidance that the new high-pressure cartridge technology was compatible with Bergara’s existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles.

The 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak, introduced this summer, has the same dimensions as a traditional brass 6.5 Creedmoor cartridge and was specifically designed by Federal Ammunition for use in existing 6.5 Creed rifles. It is not a new cartridge, but an improvement to an existing cartridge. This unusual dynamic is part of the regulatory and potential liability concerns currently vexing the gun industry.

Federal’s proprietary steel-alloy case technology contains higher pressures that drive bullets at faster speeds for better performance. Plus Peak operates at 80,000 psi, compared to about 62,000 to 65,000 psi for brass 6.5 Creed cartridges. This is possible because the Peak Alloy case material that Federal has developed is much stronger than brass. It acts as a containment vessel insulating the action and barrel from the extra pressure without inflicting extra stress on the rifle.

Related: They Built a Rifle Cartridge That Could Change Hunting and Shooting Forever

The ammunition has undergone substantial testing by Federal, a dozen gunmakers and component companies, the U.S. military (which has ordered 40 million cases), and other industry professionals.

And indeed, Bergara rifles were originally deemed “compatible” with this 6.5 Creed Plus Peak ammo, according to previous guidance from Federal and media coverage of the ammo’s launch in June. But on Oct. 29 Bergara announced that the new ammunition “is NOT approved for use in any Bergara rifle chambered in 6.5 Creedmoor.”

“These cartridge pressures haven’t been evaluated under SAAMI’s standard development process,” reads the statement, referring to the Sporting Arms and Ammunition Manufacturing Institute. “Ammunition operating outside SAAMI pressure standards for its caliber should not be used in any rifle unless Bergara formally confirms compatibility for that model.”

SAAMI sets the gun industry’s self-imposed guardrails with voluntary performance standards for firearms, ammunition, and suppressors. Bergara did not cite any incidents of catastrophic failures or injury related to shooting the new case technology through its rifles. (No one we spoke to for this story reported being aware of a catastrophic failure while testing the Plus Peak in existing 6.5 Creed rifles.)

Bergara announced in 2025 it would launch rifles in 7mm Backcountry, which debuted the same high-pressure cartridge technology as the Plus Peak. Various online distributors and retailers indicate Bergara’s HMR Pro and MG Lite models would be available in the 7BC, but the company doesn’t currently offer any rifles chambered in 7BC on its website. It’s unclear if any preproduction or production models were ever manufactured. Bergara declined to comment for this story.

In a June statement, SAAMI acknowledged the development of the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak and the creation of a working group — at Federal’s request — to evaluate the new case technology on “performance, pressure, testing, and interchangeability standards.”

SAAMI and its member companies are also discussing “the technical issues and opportunities presented by advanced cartridge case technologies and their interaction with existing firearms and suppressors.”

The organization stopped short of taking a position on the ammunition and instead urged hunters and shooters to contact their rifle’s manufacturer before shooting Federal’s 6.5 Creed Plus Peak.

“At this time, SAAMI’s guidance stays the same,” SAAMI’s senior director of technical affairs, Alan Serven, tells Outdoor Life. “As this is not a standardized cartridge, we can’t really say anything definitive about it, so we are directing folks to the manufacturer of their firearm. And we do caution that an answer from one manufacturer cannot be interpreted as a blanket statement … It’s on a case-by-case basis with each of the manufacturers and potentially down to model.”

On Nov. 5 the sporting-goods retailer Scheels sent an internal memo to its employees announcing any Plus Peak ammo should not be stocked on store floors or made available to customers.

“As of yesterday morning we have added a ‘DNS’ Do not Stock rating to all SKUs of Federal 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak ammunition,” reads the memo, obtained by Outdoor Life. “The Kinetic Group [Federal’s parent company], gun manufacturers and SAAMI are continuing to review this product. Once we have more information on their review and the stocking of the 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak, we will make a determination on how to proceed.”

Scheels has put all direct orders for Plus Peak on hold, according to the memo. The retailer also instructed its ammo and gun teams to accept any returns from customers and put any received Plus Peak “somewhere where we can ensure it will not make its way to the floor to be sold.” When Outdoor Life called a Scheels store asking about Plus Peak availability and the possibility of ordering any, the employee who answered the phone replied that no, this was not possible because “corporate put the kibosh on this until SAAMI figures out the safety and pressure issues.” The store policy affects all five SKUs of Plus Peak.

“Federal’s Peak Alloy case technology is the wave of the future for rifle ammunition,” Kinetic Group’s VP of marketing Jason Nash tells Outdoor Life, noting that Federal sought to work with as many rifle makers as possible in its development of high-pressure loads. “Over the past seven years Federal has manufactured millions of rounds in multiple cartridges and firearms. Federal has the utmost confidence in this new technology and its function in today’s firearms.”

Some gunmakers have confirmed to OL that all their existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles are compatible with Plus Peak and they have no safety concerns whatsoever. Others have remained quiet on the advisability of using Plus Peak in their 6.5 Creedmoor rifles. Bergara remains the only rifle maker who has publicly disallowed its use in its bolt-action rifles. (Break-action rifles from CVA, Bergara’s sister company, are also not approved for Plus Peak, according to Federal’s site.)

This about-face on the use of Plus Peak in Bergara rifles raises many more questions than it answers. For instance, is this hesitancy around the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak rooted in legitimate concerns of consumer safety? Or is it ultimately driven by the potential threat that some companies see to their bottom lines? Here’s what to know.

Although the conversations around Plus Peak in SAAMI’s working group are confidential, industry insiders are keyed into the crux of the issue. Here are two of the primary camps.

No one seems to be disputing the performance gains of the Plus Peak ammo. Instead, the debate is over how to standardize it.

“It definitely is an interesting idea what Federal is doing,” says one gun manufacturer who spoke on the condition of anonymity. “The industry itself is not politicking against it. The industry is not against new technology. We know and understand new products are what drive business. That’s not the issue. There’s probably some ammunition companies that are bitter or angry they didn’t think of it first, but that’s also neither here nor there.”

According to this source, the problem is the new technology currently puts too much onus on gunmakers without the opportunity to earn revenue. In fact, it poses a big expense for bigger manufacturers.

“For those of us who played by the rules and built guns to SAAMI’s standards, now we have an ammunition out there that’s outside that spec. And we’re at a conundrum to say, ‘Is it safe or not safe in our guns,’” says the gunmaker. “So one [option is] we have to go back and retest all our guns at an immense cost to verify those guns will pass these pressures.”

Some guns will inevitably pass, they say, but other guns may not. This could result in a laundry list of liability concerns in which gun companies must painstakingly note — online and in owners manuals — which rifle models are compatible and incompatible with Plus Peak.

The biggest fear is that a customer will get hurt. It’s also a bad look for any existing guns that don’t pass, given that it “may expose ‘weaknesses’ that don’t exist” in rifles that were built to spec for standard 6.5 Creed loads.

Another option for gunmakers is to simply not retest guns and not comment on (or in Bergara’s case, publicly come out against) the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak.

“It does kind of stand in the face of why we have SAAMI and why we self regulate,” says the source. “So it’s a legitimate concern. We won’t approve [Plus Peak] either. Will our guns pass it? Most likely. But we’re not chomping at the bit to go through all the testing to verify that. We have other things we need to worry about.”

The right way to have handled this new ammo technology, in this gunmaker’s mind, was to have rolled out a new cartridge that could have more easily received SAAMI approval and allowed gunmakers to collaborate on new rifle builds.

“We would have all happily engaged in a new caliber that did this, like the 7 Backcountry. We knew the pressures, and we could build a gun specifically to handle that caliber with those higher pressures. No problem. It’s the retro-fitting for everybody. Why should we go to a pile of work? Just build a new caliber. It’s a very cool technology. We’re all about that. We’re not dragging our feet because we’re old and crotchety. We’re trying to protect our business and this industry.”

In that scenario this ammunition wouldn’t be a 6.5 Creedmoor using Plus Peak technology — it would be a whole new cartridge called, for instance, the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak. Of course, this would require shooters to buy a new rifle.

“The difference is meaningful,” says Outdoor Life shooting editor John Snow of this hypothetical scenario. “And presumably, you’d have to have firearms marked with that new cartridge name in order to be in compliance with what SAAMI has deemed acceptable.”

Federal has treated the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak not as a new cartridge, but an improvement on existing 6.5 Creed ammo. The difference, again, is that it contains higher-than-SAAMI-approved pressures that drive bullets at higher velocities.

These souped-up loads give hunters and shooters the ability to get 6.5 PRC-like performance out of their existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles.

To some, the nature of Plus Peak is so monumental that industry disruption is natural and even necessary.

“We are embracing high-pressure technology like Peak Alloy,” says Sean Murphy, director of sales and marketing for Proof Research (and an occasional OL contributor). “It’s a development similar to switching from black to smokeless powder.”

Some of the more nimble gunmakers, like Proof, have already tackled the testing they need to feel comfortable about giving customers approval to run the higher-pressure load in its guns. That’s because Proof predicts this high-pressure ammunition is going to be a major player in the future, and the company is working to develop barrel and rifle technology to complement it by maximizing accuracy and performance.

“Proof Research has extensively tested Federal Peak Alloy cases in multiple cartridges, including 6.5 Creedmoor and 7 Backcountry,” says Murphy. “In 6.5 Creedmoor alone, thousands of rounds have been fired through hunting, target, and semi-automatic precision rifles with no case failures or firearm damage. Additionally, many thousands of rounds have been fired by Proof with Peak Alloy 5.56 ammunition, also without issue … Chamber-bore obstruction testing across every Proof Research receiver design showed Peak Alloy cases outperforming equivalent brass cases, with a greater safety margin at 80,000 psi than brass cases at 60,000 psi.”

The Montana-based rifle maker is also one of the most advanced barrel makers in the country. It spent years developing its PXT barrel, which Proof says accomodates much higher pressures and velocities without shortening the barrel’s lifespan.

“During our PXT barrel testing and development, more than 10,000 rounds of 7 Backcountry were fired — including 5,000 and 3,500 on individual receivers — with no damage, and wear levels comparable to brass-cased ammunition,” says Murphy.

The company also issued an apparent rebuttal to Bergara’s announcement of Oct. 29 with posts to Instagram and Facebook that same day, declaring full support and formal endorsement of Peak Alloy cartridges in its rifles.

“We have tested all our rifles — and many from other manufacturers — extensively with the Plus Peak ammunition and it’s proven to be completely safe,” says Murphy.

As part of its development and testing of the Plus Peak, Federal worked with a dozen gunmakers and related businesses. Federal confirmed to American Rifleman this summer that certain rifles from five of those companies are compatible with Plus Peak: Christensen Arms, Weatherby, Seekins Precision, Geissele, and Nemo Arms. Federal matches guidance from SAAMI that hunters and shooters should contact their gunmaker to specify which models are approved.

“Our testing has shown 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak operates in a variety of 6.5 Creedmoor firearm platforms,” Federal advises in the Plus Peak guide on its website. “Refer to your rifle manufacturer for recommendations on compatibility with high-pressure ammunition.”

Christensen Arms echoes similar language on its website. However, all existing Christensen Arms rifles chambered in 6.5 Creedmoor are compatible with the Plus Peak ammo, a spokesperson confirmed to Outdoor Life. The additional seven brands who worked with Federal include Area 419, Forster Products, Proof Research, Pure Precision, Hill Country Rifles, Short Action Customs, and Springfield Armory.

SAAMI has good cause to be extra cautious, points out Snow, since it’s a voluntary organization. The gun industry has regulated itself for a century — the result of a handshake deal with the government after World War I — and would like to continue avoiding federal oversight “like that of the auto industry,” says Snow.

But one reason gunmakers may come out against Plus Peak in their rifles has nothing to do with safety, cost, or liability.

“It’s possible that companies could see poor accuracy with the Plus Peak ammo and not recommend it,” says another industry insider, who also spoke on the condition of anonymity. “High chamber pressures, especially with some barrels and rifling designs, can cause accuracy problems that aren’t safety issues.”

Federal has patented its high-pressure cartridge technology, which means other ammunition makers can’t leverage it without paying for the privilege.

“There are rival companies that would arguably like to see this new and innovative technology put into a box and shoved off to the side,” says Snow. “Because they don’t have the ability to capitalize on this trend currently, and it’s potentially bad for their business if Plus Peak succeeds.”

The Peak Alloy case technology used in the 6.5 Creed Plus Peak was pioneered in the relatively new 7mm Backcountry, which debuted in January 2025 and was rooted in development of high-pressure ammunition for the military.

When the 7mm Backcountry was introduced, we tested more of it than anyone (besides Federal’s engineers) by shooting every SKU and running it in a dozen rifles. Although the 7BC delivered on its promise of velocity and produced accuracy equivalent to factory ammo in other cartridges, we encountered one major problem with almost every rifle. After 40 to 120 rounds, the 7BC rifles developed very stiff primary and secondary extraction. It turned out this was a common issue for other testers and shooters, too.

This issue, ultimately, was rooted in the case dimensions of the cartridge — not poorly cut chambers, as some people have speculated. After receiving updated 7BC ammo and testing the new batches in rifles that were previously showing these issues, we’ve found that the extraction issues have been resolved.

Those initial extraction issues in the 7BC were — erroneously — interpreted as effects of the increased pressure of the cartridge on the guns themselves. That doesn’t appear to be the case. In reality, most rifles chambered in 7mm Backcountry aren’t constructed differently from similar models designed for brass-cased cartridges like the 6.5 Creed.

So in other words, concerns over pressure issues from the new cartridge materials from the 7mm Backcountry to the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak appear largely unfounded.

The 7 Backcountry was accepted by SAAMI without the current speed bumps, in part, because it’s an entirely new cartridge. It required brand-new guns manufactured specifically for the cartridge. Gunmakers could either opt to produce a gun chambered in 7BC — or not. Federal’s choice to offer the Plus Peak in an existing cartridge was a different route, but a calculated one.

“The reason Federal went with the 6.5 Creedmoor is because the 6.5 Creed was introduced in 2007 and is only available in modern firearms that are of recent vintage,” says Snow. “So it is extraordinarily unlikely, I would almost say impossible, that we would see, for example, a Plus Peak .308 or .223 offered commercially. That’s because there are way too many actions and rifles of older vintages that cannot be vouched for.”

Serven could not provide a timeline on when SAAMI’s Plus Peak working group might disclose its findings.

“Our primary mission is safety, so we are proceeding with that in mind. That concern is the overriding factor,” says Serven. “So we are not in a hurry. The priority is that we get the right answer, not a fast answer.”

SAAMI’s acceptance of new cartridges standardizes the case and load across ammo makers. The organization’s blessing is not required for the sale and use of ammunition, and many niches of the hunting, shooting, and military world operate outside the bounds of SAAMI approval. This includes everyone from brands like Buffalo Bore Ammunition to wildcat cartridge developers to the federal government itself. It also means you can still get your hands on 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak.

Federal currently offers the Terminal Ascent Plus Peak for sale on its site at a limit of two boxes per customer. The four other loads are “currently unavailable.” Plenty of YouTubers have been able to get their hands on the ammo, and none we’ve seen have reported safety issues.

“The stakes are pretty consequential,” says Snow of how SAAMI decides to handle the Plus Peak ammo. “On the one hand, are we going to move with less friction and resistance into the era of high-pressure, high-performance cartridges? Or are there going to be speed bumps and other impediments put in place that effectively throttle or discourage the adoption of this new technology?”

Read Next: I Shot the First Elk With the New 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak Ammo. It’s an Undeniable Elk Hunting Cartridge

The push to adopt high-pressure rounds has come from the U.S. military, which has mandated it to maintain an advantage over “near peer” adversaries (such as Russia and China) on the battlefield. The military wants rifles capable of defeating newer body armor, and the way to do that is to drive bullets from existing guns at greater speeds.

“The government has been crystal clear about what they want and expect,” says Snow, “which is that high-pressure cartridges are happening and need to be here sooner rather than later.”

Natalie Krebs is the Executive Editor of Outdoor Life, where she tackles everything from reporting digital features to producing podcast episodes. Originally from Missouri, she currently lives in northwest Arkansas with her bird dog, Hatchet.

Tyler Freel is a Staff Writer for Outdoor Life. He lives in Fairbanks, Alaska and has been covering a variety of topics for OL for more than a decade. From backpack sheep hunting adventure stories to DIY tips to gear and gun reviews, he covers it all with a perspective that’s based in experience.

STAT+: Pharma is a ‘mixed bag’ when providing drug access to low- and middle-income countries, analysis finds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Ed’s stories explore prescription drug pricing, affordability and access, as well issues surrounding patents, litigation, and legislation. He is also the author of the morning Pharmalittle newsletter and the afternoon Pharmalot newsletter.

Many large pharmaceutical companies are taking steps to improve access to their medicines in low- and middle-income countries, but progress has been mixed and more action is needed to ensure wide availability, a new analysis found.

Notably, more drugmakers are planning to reach patients before a product launch — access plans now cover 69% of late-stage pipeline candidates compared with 18% in 2018. And across the pipeline, 95% of late-stage experimental drugs developed with a nonprofit partner have an access plan in place, versus 67% without such a partner.

Another bright spot involves cancer treatments. Access plans now cover 65% of cancer drugs in product pipelines, up from 5% in 2018. But 43% of access plans for non-communicable diseases — specifically, sickle cell disease, diabetes, and mental health — focus solely on regulatory registrations and primarily in wealthy countries.

Solana Changelog Puts Faster Web3.js Builds and Validator Work in Focus

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Solana's November 5 developer changelog highlights a collection of tooling and validator improvements rather than one headline-grabbing network upgrade. Among them are faster builds for JavaScript applications, changes affecting validator operations and further work around execution tools.

Solana's November 5 developer changelog highlights a collection of tooling and validator improvements rather than one headline-grabbing network upgrade. Among them are faster builds for JavaScript applications, changes affecting validator operations and further work around execution tools. For developers, these smaller improvements can influence how quickly applications ship and how reliably infrastructure behaves. For investors, the distinction is that a published development update is not necessarily a newly activated change on mainnet.

One item in the changelog concerns the JavaScript SDK build process using Rolldown. The developers describe a reduction in an internal build benchmark from around two seconds to almost zero. That is a promising productivity claim, but it applies to the specified workflow and benchmark rather than every Solana app suddenly loading faster for users. Faster library builds can improve testing and release cycles, particularly for teams maintaining many packages. Its end-user effect depends on whether downstream developers adopt the updated tools and whether the gains carry over to their own projects.

The update also points to work on the Agave validator client, typed interfaces and elements of Firedancer leader handover. These terms describe software and operational changes within the infrastructure that keeps the chain running. Their practical significance lies in reliability, interoperability and the conditions under which different validator implementations coordinate. However, a changelog reference should not be treated as evidence that every validator has deployed the work or that a planned network feature has passed all activation checks. Operators need specific release notes, configuration guidance and rollout schedules.

The changelog includes improvements involving Anchor, RPC-related tools and local development environments such as Surfpool. Anchor is widely used for building Solana programs, while RPC services provide the interface through which wallets, apps and analytics products query the chain. Small optimizations in account allocation or local testing can save developer time and reduce avoidable mistakes. They do not remove the need for security review, careful transaction handling or capacity planning. Because Solana applications rely on several layers, a weakness at the RPC or front-end level can still affect users even when core consensus works normally.

Solana has long emphasized throughput and latency, but engineering benchmarks depend on hardware, software versions and the specific task measured. An SDK build benchmark is not a transaction-per-second statistic, while validator handover work is not a promise of zero outages. Keeping those categories separate helps readers understand what the team has actually improved. Adoption may eventually show up in developer retention, easier deployment and more dependable applications, but those effects take time and should be measured rather than assumed from a release note.

Developers can follow the linked repositories, testing results and future rollout notices for each change. The clearest evidence of impact would be adoption in major tools, reproducible performance measurements and fewer operational headaches for app builders and node operators. The November update reflects continuous ecosystem maintenance—often less dramatic than a large protocol announcement, but essential to whether Solana can support more demanding financial applications over time.

Primary source: Solana developer changelog. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s recent review of Solana’s October development milestones.

Persona 4 Revival Wasn't Meant to Be Part of Packed February 2027 Release Schedule

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Persona 4 Revival was initially designed to launch for PS5 in the late summer months of this year, not the very crowded February 2027 release calendar it's now found itself in.

In an interview with 3DMGAME, chief producer Kazuhisa Wada shared the original plan was to launch the remake "as early as possible — we originally planned to release it about six months earlier, which would have been just in time for summer, but we ultimately couldn't make it that way".

The original Persona 4 often sparks memories of summer for players, as a large part of the game takes place during the season and includes trips to the beach and festivals specific to that part of the year. Putting the PS5 remake out during that same season would have made a lot of sense, but the Atlus executive claims the firm "couldn't make it that way".

It's not clear whether Wada is referring to development issues or a different problem that affected the release.

Either way, Persona 4 Revival now launches on 18th February 2027, a month full of other big PS5 releases. The delay to God of War Laufey gives everything else a touch more breathing room, but the schedule still looks like this:

As an RPG, its strongest competition is probably Fable, which releases just five days after Persona 4 Revival. The Tomb Raider: Legacy of Atlantis remake should take up a lot of mindshare in the weeks before it, too.

We shall still play it in February, but getting to experience it again during those quieter summer months would have been bliss.

Liam grew up with a PlayStation controller in his hands and a love for Metal Gear Solid. Nowadays, he's found playing the latest and greatest PS5 games as well as supporting Derby County. That last detail is his downfall.

Yikes, three remakes in one month. I'll only be getting Atelier, thanks.

I'm looking forward to this, but I'm worried about the voice acting.

"Always Ready. Tailored Control. Pros' Arsenal."

NACON has announced today the availability of its REVO range of Xbox controllers, three officially licensed pads. The trio aims to accommodate the needs of every Xbox and PC player, because "every gamer has a style, every style has a controller."

The REVO X and REVO X MAX are available for purchase today, with the REVO X PRO arriving sometime in 2027. All three controllers are designed for different player styles, and so we've listed the price point and details for each pad. Here's how NACON described the REVO range:

"The new range has been designed to meet the specific needs of every type of player, from the casual gamer looking for the immediate efficiency of a "Plug-and-Play" controller, to the esports expert seeking deep customization."

The REVO controller is the embodiment of accessible and reliable performance. It is ideal for players looking for fluid control and flawless durability.

Announced for 2027, the REVO PRO is designed for the most demanding competitive players. It combines the best features of the range, including instant micro-switch triggers and programmable rear buttons, to offer optimized responsiveness and control for competition.

For players who demand complete customization, the REVO MAX offers advanced adjustment options to adapt the controller to their playstyle; these options are in addition to all the REVO features detailed above.

Bees make a natural plastic that starts breaking down in soil within months

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Solitary bees make natural plastic that breaks down in soil, offering clues for designing less persistent synthetic materials.

A plastic wrapper may be useful for a few minutes, then stay in the environment for centuries. But a small bee makes a plastic-like material that starts breaking down in soil within months.

The bee, Colletes inaequalis, uses it to cover the inside of its underground nest. The material helps protect the young bee growing inside.

Researchers studied these nests in Tennessee. In fresh nests, plastic made up 73% of the lining’s weight, but four months later, that share had fallen to 40%.

Now the team wants to learn how the bee makes a material that is tough but still breaks down. The answer could help scientists make better plastics for everyday use.

Jonathan Coote, an assistant professor at Virginia Tech, led the study.

Laura Russo, who studies bees at the University of Tennessee, first told him about the material while he was a doctoral student there.

Coote kept studying it during his later research at the University of Minnesota. After joining Virginia Tech in 2025, he made it his main research focus.

Scientists identified the chemical makeup of the bee’s plastic more than 45 years ago. But for years afterward, few people studied it further.

Most bees live alone rather than in hives. This species, common in the eastern United States, digs tunnels in the ground and builds small rooms for its young.

The female covers each room with a thin, clear layer, which gives these insects the name cellophane bees. She adds pollen and nectar, lays one egg, and closes the room.

The young bee spends more than 90% of its life inside. The lining likely protects it and its food from water and fungus.

The lining contains silk and a natural plastic made by the female.

A gland connected to her stinger releases tiny, ring-shaped molecules, which join into long chains as she spreads them over the walls.

The bee’s material resembles polyethylene, a plastic widely used in packaging.

Polyethylene contains long chains of carbon atoms held together by strong bonds that are hard to break, even in industrial compost.

Only about 9% of the world’s plastic was recycled in 2022. Nearly a third is poorly managed and ends up in the environment, where it can break into tiny pieces called microplastics.

Scientists have tried making similar plastics with weaker links in their chains. Water or enzymes, natural substances that help chemical reactions happen, can break these links.

These materials are called polyesters, and the bee’s plastic belongs to this group. “This is a polyester that mimics polyethylene,” Coote said.

Even with weaker links, most lab-made versions barely break down.

Their chains pack closely together and keep water out, making the weaker links hard to reach.

The best-studied version lost only 18% of its weight after 280 days in industrial compost. It did not break down at all under milder conditions.

The bee’s nests offered a clue that its material was different. More than 10,000 nests can share one site, and bees may return to the same ground for decades.

Yet the old plastic linings do not seem to pile up. Something appears to break them down.

The team first tried to dissolve the bee’s plastic to study its chains. After two days in a solvent heated to 302°F (150°C), the material still held its shape and size.

This suggested that the chains were linked together like a net.

About 60% of the plastic also formed tightly packed areas called crystals, arranged like those in polyethylene.

Usually, these tightly packed areas make plastic harder to break down. So the bee’s material seemed to have the same features that cause trouble in lab-made plastics.

The researchers collected fresh nests in March 2023 in Hamblen County, eastern Tennessee.

They returned in July and October 2023 and February 2024, digging seven to 12 inches (17 to 30 centimeters) down to find them.

After four months, the linings could still bend and hold their shape. By about seven months, when the young bees had become adults, cracks had appeared.

By 11 months, the linings crumbled when handled roughly. They had not vanished, but they were falling apart.

At first, the lining was about 73% plastic and 27% silk by weight. After four months, plastic made up only 40% of the remaining lining.

Silk usually breaks down fairly quickly, so the team was surprised to find the plastic breaking down even faster.

Later, the balance changed less, suggesting both materials were then breaking down at roughly the same pace.

Tightly packed regions accounted for about 60% of the plastic initially and nearly 100% of the remaining plastic after four months. By 11 months, their share had fallen to 39%.

This suggests the looser parts broke down first. The tightly packed parts appear to have broken down later.

The answer may lie in the plastic’s tiny building blocks. Most are rings with 18 carbon atoms, while some have 20 or 22.

Mixing these sizes may spread the weaker links unevenly along the chains. That could leave more links where water and enzymes can reach them.

The tightly packed areas are also small and less orderly, which could help. Microbes in the nest may also play a part.

Another idea involves an enzyme in the bee’s saliva. It might help build the plastic, then help break it down later.

The researchers rinsed the linings three times and collected the liquid. They added a chemical that this kind of enzyme can break apart.

Liquid from both a new lining and a four-month-old lining broke down the chemical. This suggests enzymes could keep working in the nest for months.

But the test used only one new nest and one older nest. More tests are needed to find out what those enzymes actually do.

The team still does not know which explanation matters most. Each nest provides only a few milligrams of lining, so some tests ran just once or twice, and all the older nests came from one site.

“The goal is to be able to answer that and then engineer it into something that we can make at scale. I don’t see a future where we have these massive bee farms making all of our plastic products,” Coote said.

If the spacing of the weaker links is the key, scientists could try copying it. They want a useful plastic that can break down in soil after its job is done.

The study is published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

Schumer accuses Trump of defending Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang at cost of national security

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Democratic leader Sen. Charles Schumer ripped President Donald Trump on Tuesday for defending embattled Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang at the expense of U.S. national security.

"The press reports that Trump was briefed more than a year ago that Nvidia's CEO was having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, and Trump didn't do anything – anything – about it," Schumer, D-N.Y., said on the Senate floor.

"Can you believe that?" said Schumer, the Senate's minority leader.

Schumer's broadside came on the heels of New York Times reports that Trump had been briefed about Huang's extramarital affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang — whom U.S. intelligence concluded was a spy for China — and opted not to do anything about it.

Trump suggested to reporters on Monday that he only recently learned of the allegations about Alice Huang, and the president commented Monday that he "really" believes Jensen Huang's claim to him directly that he had no idea Alice Huang was a spy.

"One of our most important companies that is involved in national security, and the CEO might be having an affair with a Chinese spy, and Trump did nothing about it," Schumer said Tuesday.

"What a dereliction of duty. He's supposed to protect America, not China, not his friends," Schumer said. "Trump has been busy trying to defend Jensen Huang when he ought to be defending our national security. It's that simple, it's obvious."

Schumer's comments escalated growing bipartisan concern in Congress about the Nvidia CEO, whose company makes advanced chips that power artificial intelligence in the U.S. and China's race to dominate that technology.

Schumer noted that Trump earlier this year invited Huang to join him on a state visit to Beijing, and that he took Huang's advice by granting the People's Republic of China "access to some of our most advanced AI chips."

Nvidia also donated $25 million to Trump's controversial White House ballroom project, the Democrat said.

"Despite talking a big game, Trump continues to show a troubling resistance to confront China on AI and to protect American national security," Schumer said. "I've been saying it for years: our cutting-edge AI chips are too valuable to fall into the hands of potential adversaries."

Schumer in his speech called on Republicans to join Democrats in passing several pieces of legislation — the AI OVERWATCH Act, the MATCH Act, and the Chip Security Act — which he said would "cut off China's access to advanced American technology."

"We also need answers on Nvidia's influence campaign and how [it] affected the Trump administration's national security decisions, including the loosening – the loosening – of key export controls," Schumer said. "We need a full accounting for Trump's failure to address foreign espionage in the American tech industry."

CNBC has requested comment from the White House about Schumer's remarks.

The remarks came nearly a week after Democrats won enough Senate and House races nationwide to take majority control of both chambers in January. Schumer hopes to be selected majority leader.

Regardless of which Democrats are tapped to serve as majority leaders in both chambers, the party will have the ability to launch investigations into Nvidia and Huang.

Earlier Tuesday, Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., said on CNBC's "Squawk Box" that Congress needs to "get to the bottom" of Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang.

Moolenaar, who chairs the House Permanent Select Committee on China, said he wants to know whether Huang shared privileged information with a Chinese spy; whether Huang was influenced to lobby against preventing chip sales to China; and whether China was given preference in purchasing Nvidia's limited supply of advanced chips.

And he urged a thorough internal investigation of Huang.

"If it becomes less of an investigation, more of damage control, that's not going to work because people have to have confidence," Moolenaar said.

"We're dealing with the country, the Chinese Communist Party that is a foremost adversary, that's funding wars and supporting Russia, supporting Iran, North Korea," he said. "And we have basically a Cold War race with technology. We cannot allow the premier company at the forefront of this to, in some way, be irresponsible."

Congressional scrutiny of Huang's affair has intensified since members returned to Washington on Monday, a day after Nvidia announced it was opening an independent investigation of Jensen Huang's conduct.

Rep. Jay Obernolte, R-Calif., co-chair of the House AI task force, raised concerns that the scandal could plague talks over export controls for advanced AI chips."It's important not to have a personal scandal interfere with the substantive policy discussion about to what degree we should be allowing the export of cutting edge chips to our geopolitical competitors like China," Obernolte told CNBC. "I hope this isn't a distraction because that's a very important conversation that needs to happen."

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., said in a press conference on Monday that "we're going to need a full briefing specifically as it relates to the Nvidia issue so we can all get to the bottom of what has happened and what the implications are in terms of national security"

Moolenaar co-authored a letter with Florida Rep. Brian Mast and Arkansas Rep. Rick Crawford on Nov. 5 asking Huang to appear before the Select Committee on China, the House Foreign Affairs Committee and House Select Committee on Intelligence. Mast chairs the foreign affairs panel, and Crawford chairs intelligence.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the three Republicans wrote.

Mast said Tuesday he did not know whether Huang had responded to the letter he authored with Moolenar and Crawford demanding the tech CEO brief them. "I can't say that I've heard an answer back yet that," Mast told reporters at the Capitol.Mast then rattled off a list of questions he has for Huang, including where the company has been selling chips, what Megaspeed may have been using them for, who has been remotely accessing Nvidia chips and what information was passed between Huang and the potential spy. "Obviously questions relating to his relationship and what information was or was not passed about anything," Mast said when asked what he wants answered.

Mast said he "can't say it would hurt" to hear from the Commerce Department as well, noting he speaks regularly to the Bureau of Industry and Security. "So that's obviously something that falls directly under Commerce," he said

Aros museum in Aarhus, Denmark acquires sun-like Olafur Eliasson sculpture in new partnership with the artist

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Olafur Eliasson's Presence (2025), installed at the Queensland Art Gallery and Gallery of Modern Art in Brisbane

Photo: Chloë Callistemon © QAGOMA Courtesy of the artist; neugerriemschneider, Berlin; and Tanya Bonakdar Gallery, New York © 2025 Olafur Eliasson

The Aros Aarhus Art Museum in eastern Denmark will acquire a major piece by the high-profile Danish-Icelandic artist Olafur Eliasson—a crowd-pleasing vast glowing yellow sphere that appears to hover in space (Presence, 2025)—as part of a new long-term partnership.

The initiative between the artist and the museum, called “Shared Futures”, also encompasses Thin Ice, a solo exhibition to open autumn 2027. “This exhibition presents key works from recent decades alongside new projects reflecting Eliasson’s growing engagement with the climate, ecology, and humanity’s relationship with its surroundings,” says a statement.

A new site-specific version of Eliasson’s installation Waterfall (2004) will meanwhile be installed on Aros’s Art Square from 9 October next year; Studio Olafur Eliasson will also create a publicly accessible library collection of books on Eliasson’s artistic production at Aros under the plans. In addition, the partnership includes a dedicated postdoctoral research position in collaboration with Aarhus University.

Eliasson's Waterfall (2019), installed at Tate Modern in London

Photo: Anders Sune Berg; Courtesy of the artist; neugerriemschneider, Berlin; Tanya Bonakdar Gallery, New York © 2019 Olafur Eliasson

Eliasson says in a statement: “Aros has supported my work in Denmark for many years, so it felt natural to make my online archive and library available there… the ambition is to show that museums can offer depth and focus for engaging with an artist’s practice in a more detailed, granular way.” The museum is also home to Eliasson’s rainbow rooftop walkway Your Rainbow Panorama (2011).

Eliasson's Your rainbow panorama (2011) at Aros

Tina Sørensen © Schmidt Hammer Lassen, 2026

Rebecca Matthews, the museum's director, tells The Art Newspaper: “I first met Olafur Eliasson when I was chief executive of the European Capital of Culture in 2017, when he created an extraordinary stage design for the performance Tree of Codes. We were supposed to have our photograph taken together, but it was pouring with rain, so we ended up standing in a colonnade, talking. I remember thinking: If I ever get to lead a museum, he will be the first person I call.” Matthews became director of Aros in 2022.

In December 2025, Eliasson appeared on The Art Newspaper podcast A brush with…. with Ben Luke. “His works can be deceptively simple or enormously complex, but often share a rigorous and reductive geometry, which may conversely produce expansive and multifarious perceptual, sensory and embodied effects,” wrote Luke.

Site-specific installation called Life opened this week at the Fondation Beyeler near Basel in Switzerland and can be visited at all hours

Artist's Tate Modern retrospective features works drawn largely from European collections in an effort to reduce carbon footprint

Olafur Eliasson talks to Ben Luke about his influences—from writers to musicians, film-makers and, of course, other artists—and the cultural experiences that have shaped his life and work

Arsenal transfer news: ‘Ideal’ January signing revealed as Mikel Arteta draws up four-man shortlist

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Oliver Thomas | 10 Nov 2026 12:51

Arsenal have drawn up a shortlist of four attacking targets ahead of the January transfer window, according to a report.

Mikel Arteta was unable to bolster his frontline during the summer after steering the Gunners to their first Premier League title in over two decades last season.

A big-money move to the Emirates Stadium for the likes of Vinicius Junior and Julian Alvarez failed to materialise, while Morgan Rogers and Bradley Barcola joined Chelsea and Liverpool respectively for fees in excess of £100m.

A plethora of attack-minded players have since been linked with Arsenal in recent months, with Arteta particularly keen to bring in a new left-sided forward after replacing Gabriel Martinelli and Leandro Trossard with only Christos Tzolis in the summer.

According to the Independent, a mid-season incoming at Arsenal may hinge on the potential departure of midfielder Martin Zubimendi amid reported interest from Real Madrid, Atletico Madrid and Chelsea.

Should Zubimendi’s exit materialise, the Gunners would be expected to use the funds generated to recruit a new winger, with Alex Baena, Endrick, Antonio Nusa and El Junior Kroupi all viewed as potential January signings.

As things stand, Bournemouth star Kroupi is regarded as Arsenal’s ‘ideal candidate’, but they are aware that the Cherries are unlikely to entertain negotiations until next summer.

Kroupi ended last season as Bournemouth’s top scorer, netting 13 Premier League goals for a side who secured their highest-ever top-flight finish (sixth) and qualified for the Europa League for the first time in their history.

The versatile 20-year-old forward, who has primarily operated in a central position at Bournemouth, has only just recovered from a long-term injury, featuring for just 57 minutes across four substitute outings this term.

Kroupi is under contract at the Vitality Stadium until June 2030 and it is thought that Bournemouth would demand a fee in excess of £80m for youngster, who has also been linked with Tottenham Hotspur and Barcelona.

Aware that prising Kroupi away from Bournemouth in January is a tall order, Arsenal are said to be surveying other options, with Atletico’s Baena, Real’s Endrick and RB Leipzig’s Nusa all seen as ‘potentials’.

Arsenal may gain significant transfer leverage due to Zubimendi's potential availability, given that both Real Madrid and Atletico are said to be interested in signing him.

A proposed swap deal involving Zubimendi is thought to remain a distinct possibility, and this would still allow the Gunners to revisit a big-money deal for Kroupi further down the line.

Arsenal are in a strong position to attract Europe’s elite players as they currently sit top of the Premier League table – viewed by many as firm favourites to retain their title – and are considered as one of the frontrunners to win their first-ever Champions League trophy this term.

Here's everything you need to do to get inside the Crimson Manor in Phantom Blade Zero

Opening the Phantom Blade Zero Crimson Manor is a long and difficult process that you won't complete until you're very far into the game – basically, if you're reading this because you've just stumbled upon the manor under the Weeping Creek Valley Sha-Chi Tower, don’t worry about it for now!What makes it so complicated is that getting the necessary tongue key ties into several other side quests that will take you all over the game world, and the Crimson Manor quest itself links to an even greater over-arching side quest. But, to help you out, I've explained the entire process of getting inside the Crimson Manor – be aware that there are some story spoilers below!

To get inside the Crimson Manor, you need to get the Puppet's Tongue key by going through three hidden portal doors found across the game, all of which lead to the Three-Eyed Puppet (this is 'The Herald's Illusion' quest).

The arduous part is therefore finding those doors, especially since two of them are in far-flung regions of the game, so you need to make sure you have access to those regions first. To reach the Mandate Encampment, you need the Phantom Blade Zero Mandate Encampment Key. And to get to the Mountain of Imprisonment, you must have repaired the Illusory Pipa, which you can only do if you have found Myst in Pangzhen and thoroughly explored the Village of Lu, the Mandate Encampment, and Meigu Lake for the missing parts.

With all that in mind, here are the locations of the three secret doors:

When you go through the final door, interact with the Three-Eyed Puppet and it'll give you the necessary Puppet's Tongue, completing 'The Herald's Illusion' quest in the process, and finally giving you access to this mysterious subterranean mansion. The puppet also hands over its own Yin blue crystal eye and says that you need to find the Yang eye within the Crimson Manor, which is all for the 'Secrets at the Heart of the Lake' quest.

Anyway, fast travel to the Crimson Manor and interact with the lock to insert the tongue and open the door. Plunder the manor then head out the back and jump down the pit, carefully working your way to the bottom.

You'll be faced with a double boss fight against the Puppet Scavenger and Acolyte Hansui here. Neither are that tough, with the former's huge bulk making it slow and easy to hit and the latter being quite easy to overwhelm up close, so long as you dodge and block plenty of their ice attacks. Kill the pair and you'll get the Yang eye and Hansui's Frost Mask, thus finally completing the Crimson Manor quest!But now you've got the Yin and Yang crystal eyes and the 'Secrets at the Heart of the Lake' quest to wrap up. If you haven't already, you need to defeat the other three Acolyte boss fights found at scarecrows around the game – there's one in the Village of Lu, Pangzhen, and Lake Meigu – and take their masks to the Four Guardians' Mirror on the icy western side of Lake Meigu. This will take you to a special area where you'll eventually get to use those two crystal eyes, but only after you defeat the Blizzard Blademaster.

Watford hold talks with 48-year-old boss who has been out of work since October 2025

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Watford are sat 20th in the Championship table with 15 points from as many games. The Hornets have struggled for any sort of momentum, putting pressure on manager Alessio Dionisi despite the club hierarchy’s reported desire to give the boss more time than some of his predecessors.

Now though, a report from Romanian journalist Emanuel Rose states Watford have held talks with Edward Iordanescu over the managerial position at Vicarage Road. Contact has been described as ‘positive’, but there have not been any concrete English reports that the Championship side are considering a managerial change.

Iordanescu is available having been out of the game since leaving Legia Warsaw in October 2025. He previously managed Romania’s national team, alongside prestigious clubs such as FCSB and CFR Cluj.

Iordanescu is a 48-year-old coach who started his career in the dugout in 2010 after retiring in 2004. He was only 32 when he was first named as interim manager of Steaua Bucharest, where he stayed as an assistant coach to Ilie Stan until 2012.

He spent short spells in charge of Fortuna Brazi and FCM Targu Mures in Romania before first earning acclaim at Pandurii Targu Jiu, where his work earned him the CSKA Sofia job in Bulgaria. That stint lasted only 12 games though, and Iordanescu would return to his native in spells at Astra Giurgiu, CFR Cluj (x2), Gaz Metan and FCSB.

The rumoured Watford target managed the Romania national side from January 2022 to July 2024, winning 10 of 28 games and leading them to the UEFA Euro 2024 round of 16 before leaving at the end of the tournament.

A return to club football followed at Legia Warsaw in Poland, but they struggled in European and domestic football with Iordanescu criticised for inconsistent team selection, fruitless tactical changes, poor physical output metrics and disenchantment within the squad.

Iordanescu won the Romanian Superliga once and their Super Cup twice, and the same trophy in Poland once. His most used formation is a 4-3-3, but has also deployed his sides in a 4-2-3-1 setup.

As touched on earlier, nothing has been concretely reported by English or local outlets that Watford are planning a change in the dugout. However, rumoured talks with Iordanescu could indicate that they are staying prepared if they do sack Dionisi at the very leasy.

If there is a time to change manager, it is now. The international break offers a chance to get a new boss in and settled before the return to league action.

Iordanescu could be brought in swiftly and given time on the training ground to work with his new squad before club football returns against Sheffield United on November 21st. However, it remains to be seen if anything comes of the links, with managerial speculation never far around the corner at Vicarage Road.

Why this year’s flu season could be hitting teens and young adults hard

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Amanda Erickson is a news editor at STAT.

Get your daily dose of health and medicine every weekday with STAT’s free newsletter Morning Rounds. Sign up here.

Good morning! One of our items today concerns this year’s flu. In the course of my research, I learned that the virus is hitting teenagers and young adults especially hard. I offer one possible explanation for what’s going on below, but welcome your theories and other ideas. We’ve also got a round-up on a lot of cardiology research testing ideas that didn’t work, and why at least one doctor is excited about that.

That’s the number of people who could get access to Medicaid, thanks to last week’s blue wave. STAT’s John Wilkerson reports that three of the 10 states that haven’t expanded Medicaid could reconsider their holdouts now that Democrats have more power in state government: Texas, Georgia, and Wisconsin.

The Democratic governors-elect in all three made Medicaid expansion a part of their campaigns. But the GOP still holds majorities in the Texas and Georgia state senates, which could complicate the path forward. Read more from John.

After last weekend’s American Heart Association meeting, we know a lot about what may not work. Experts presented research showing that targeting inflammation didn’t limit serious cardiac events. Neither did reducing lipoprotein(a), a genetically-based risk factor for cardiovascular disease.

Researchers also found that lowering blood pressure in people with diabetes didn’t help cut down on heart attacks, and educating emergency responders and bystanders to improve their responses to sudden cardiac arrest didn’t keep more people alive. Even yoga didn’t seem to move the needle on health improvements after heart failure.

A slew of studies showing the kinds of interventions that don’t measurably change outcomes might seem a little disappointing. But one person STAT’s Liz Cooney spoke to offered a silver lining: “I always find that when a trial is neutral, I think it’s more exciting for scientists to find a way forward … I call it a really big, beautiful baby who shouldn’t be thrown out with the bathwater.” Read more about what doctors are taking away from the AHA conference here.

The number of flu cases is climbing, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. At least 1.7 million cases have been reported and 1,400 Americans have died so far this year, with case clusters in southern states and the West Coast.

That makes sense, pulmonologist Vin Gupta, affiliate professor at the University of Washington’s School of Medicine, told the radio station WBUR. This year’s dominant flu strain — a mutated h2N1 virus — surged earlier this year in Japan. “The West Coast has major travel gateways from Asia, and that’s what we likely think is driving this initial spike,” Gupta said.

Case counts are higher than usual for early November, but that doesn’t mean the virus will be more severe, or that peaks will continue to be higher. Another element to watch: According to one preliminary study, teenagers and young adults seem more affected by this year’s strain than older people.

This could be because our immune response is shaped in part by the flu strains we were exposed to as children. The strain of flu that’s spreading now is a mutated version of the strain that fueled the 2009 swine flu pandemic. People without additional h2N1 exposure before then may be especially vulnerable because of the particular mutations being spread, USA Today reports.

Consider this — someone could ask AI about the risks of smoking, receive completely accurate information, and still come away with the impression that lighting up isn’t all that bad for you.

How? As academics Jessica Fishman and John L. Jackson Jr. explain, a lot of health messaging comes down to emphasis. An AI-generated response could share, honestly, that most smokers never get lung cancer. It’s true, but it’s not nearly the whole story. And right now, we just don’t know exactly how LLMs are explaining health information, and how that’s impacting patients.

That needs to change, they write. As Fishman and Jackson put it: “Medical ethics pairs the duty to do no harm with beneficence, which is an affirmative obligation to do good. Safety evaluations of AI have focused mainly on the first duty … Far less attention has gone to the second: whether AI can help people by communicating more effectively. Responsible engineering means honoring both duties.” Read more.

Doctors are burnt out, exhausted, leaving the field. On Monday, the American Medical Association’s House of Delegates weighed in on a related phenomenon — moral injury. In a new policy, the AMA voted to recognize moral injury as a “distinct and significant threat” to the well-being of doctors.

The AMA distinguishes moral injury from other challenges such as exhaustion or distress, arguing that it is brought on when systemic constraints make it impossible for doctors to deliver evidence-based, equitable care.

“The practice of medicine is fundamentally a moral activity, yet physicians routinely face barriers that can prevent them from delivering the care they know their patients need,” said AMA Board Secretary Pauline P. Huynh in a press release. “When those barriers repeatedly conflict with physicians’ professional values, that harm can contribute to physicians reducing their hours, leaving positions or training programs, or exiting practice altogether.”

STAT’s First Opinion published a column on this phenomenon in 2018, which helped bring this issue to light. Take a look here.

Amanda Erickson is a news editor at STAT.

Understand how science, health policy, and medicine shape the world every day

"Undoubtedly, it would be very difficult to replicate the experience of consuming our game as intended because of technical limitations," says Take-Two boss Strauss Zelnick

Take-Two CEO Strauss Zelnick has acknowledged the influx of AI-assisted game decompilations appearing online ahead of Rockstar Games releasing GTA 6 next week.

Recent months have seen a huge increase in the prevalence of vibe-coded AI-led recompilation projects. Zelnick does acknowledge "every entertainment business that exists is subject to some element of piracy," but says: "I think it will be manageable, and we are focused on it."

Ultimately, I don't think a PC recompilation of GTA 6 will have any major effect on the game's financial performance. If it does happen, most are likely to abandon it in favor of the official PC port when that does drop, especially if it comes with GTA Online.
