A whale buried for 9.5 million years had unexpected company

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scientists discovered ancient brittle star fossils beside whale bones, revealing how marine animals gathered around carcasses millions ago.

Brittle stars gather around dead whales on today’s seafloor, sometimes at much higher densities than on the surrounding seabed. Yet no fossil had ever placed them at a whale carcass.

Studies of fossil whale carcasses have mostly reported clams, snails, and animals that eat bone.

Now fossils from beside a beaked whale skeleton in Denmark suggest that brittle stars joined these gatherings 9.5 million years ago.

If that reading holds, it’s the first fossil record of a brittle star tied to a whale fall. That’s the term scientists use for a sunken whale carcass and the community of life it feeds.

The clues are tiny skeletal plates from a species new to science, now named Ophiura huebnerjenseni. The fossils are loose pieces rather than whole animals, so they can’t show what the brittle stars ate.

Ben Thuy, curator of paleontology at Luxembourg’s National Museum of Natural History, led the study.

Thuy worked with Mette Elstrup and Trine Sørensen of Museum Sønderjylland in Denmark. The fourth author, Lea Numberger-Thuy, works at the Luxembourg museum and at a dinosaur park in Germany.

The Gram Clay Pit in southern Denmark exposes about 23 to 25 feet (7 to 7.5 meters) of clay that built up on the floor of an ancient North Sea. Geologists call these layers the Gram Formation.

The pit is famous for its fossil clams, snails, and animals with backbones, including whale skeletons and giant megalodon vertebrae. Sea urchins, sea stars, and their relatives have been far scarcer there.

Scientists call that group of spiny-skinned animals the echinoderms. Before this study, only one echinoderm species had been reported from the Gram Formation.

Brittle stars are echinoderms too, with long, thin, flexible arms built from many small skeletal pieces.

The North Sea has plenty of them today, yet none had been found in these old clays. Thuy and Numberger-Thuy had already searched for them in clays of a similar age at Groß Pampau in northern Germany, without success.

The new fossils came from sediment collected in 2018, during the excavation of a beaked whale‘s lower jaw and four vertebrae from its backbone.

The bones were found about 21 feet (6.5 meters) below the top of the exposed clay. That level dates to roughly 9.5 million years ago.

Five sediment samples came from around the skeleton. The team dried each one, soaked it in water, and washed it through screens, then washed the leftovers a second time with a detergent-like chemical.

Under a microscope, the researchers picked out the smallest fossils one by one. The best brittle-star pieces were then photographed with a scanning electron microscope.

For comparison, they also processed a sample packed with tiny fossils. It came from 2 to 4 inches (5 to 10 centimeters) below a fossil log that was dug up in 2023.

“Only three samples taken directly beside the whale bones contained brittle-star remains. Samples from the same layer farther from the skeleton, and other comparison samples, yielded none,” Thuy said.

The three samples held 33 skeletal pieces in all. Most were plates from the sides of the arms, along with one piece of an arm’s inner backbone and one plate from the central disc of the body.

The arm plate chosen as the reference specimen for the species came from a sample taken 2 to 8 inches (5 to 20 centimeters) from the whale’s jawbones. Its shape placed the animal in the genus Ophiura, which still lives in the North Sea.

The closest living match there is Ophiura albida. The fossil plates differ from it in two ways: they have a slanted upper edge, and they lack the faint spurs that O. albida plates carry.

A cluster of fossils beside a skeleton doesn’t automatically mean the animals lived there, so the team considered other explanations.

“We considered whether currents had simply concentrated the plates around the bones, or whether local chemical conditions had helped preserve them,” Thuy added.

“However, sea-urchin fragments of comparable size and mineral composition occurred in every sample studied. This makes a preservation-only explanation less likely and supports the interpretation that the brittle stars lived on or close to the whale carcass.”

Scientists divide a whale fall’s life into stages. In the early stage, the carcass enriches the seabed around it and feeds animals that take advantage of any sudden meal.

In the deep sea, later stages can follow. One of them supports life that runs on sulfur compounds released as the bones decay.

Fossils around the Gram whale included clams that filter food from the water and meat-eating snails, some of them scavengers.

The team found no clams of the kind that live off chemical energy with help from bacteria. The bones had no animals cemented to their surfaces and showed no signs of burrowing.

“We found no evidence for the later sulfide-based community stages that can develop at deep-sea whale falls,” Elstrup said.

“The discovery therefore adds a rare fossil perspective on how animals responded to a large food source on an ancient shallow-water seabed.”

Ophiura huebnerjenseni honors Holger Hübner and Thomas Jensen, who founded the heavy metal festival Wacken Open Air. It joins a long line of species named after musicians, but this one comes with a geological link.

“The clay that accumulated on the bottom of the ancient North Sea not only preserved the remains of a 9.5-million-year-old brittle star, it also turns Wacken Open Air into the muddy battlefield renowned by metalheads worldwide,” Numberger-Thuy said.

Brittle stars have been found before beside the carcasses of ichthyosaurs, ancient sea reptiles, in rocks from the Early Jurassic. A fossil whale fall had never produced one.

The researchers can’t yet say whether this species lived only at whale carcasses.

They favor the idea that it lived on the surrounding seabed and gathered at the carcass in numbers large enough to leave a fossil trace.

Living brittle stars at shallow whale falls also come from the nearby seafloor. Finding plates of this species in ordinary Gram mud, far from any bones, would support that idea.

For now, the only place Ophiura huebnerjenseni has been found is the mud right beside one whale.

The full study was published in the journal Neues Jahrbuch für Geologie und Paläontologie – Abhandlungen.

Binance Warns of Apple iOS Flaw That Could Expose iPhone Users' Private Keys

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Binance Warns of Apple iOS Flaw That Could Expose iPhone Users' Private Keys

Binance has warned iPhone users about a vulnerability in Apple's iOS that it says could let malicious apps read files stored by other apps, putting crypto wallet seed phrases, private keys and login credentials at risk.

The flaw, dubbed "worse_query," affects iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1 and 27.2, the exchange tweeted on Tuesday. "This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance," it said.

A vulnerability (worse_query) affecting iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1, and 27.2 allows malicious apps to read local files from other apps, putting seed phrases, private keys, and login credentials at risk across the entire ecosystem. This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance.…

Binance urged users to delete any apps installed from untrusted sources, such as sideloaded or manually signed app files, and to update iOS and all apps to the latest official versions. Anyone who suspects a breach should move their funds to a new wallet created on a clean device and reset all active login sessions, it said.

iPhone apps normally run inside a sandbox, which stops them from accessing other apps' private files. A researcher who goes by forcequitOS published worse_query on GitHub as a sandbox escape for iOS 26.0 through 26.7.2 and iOS 27.2, describing it as a proof of concept for developers that "isn't useful by itself." Among the locations it can reach is the folder where iOS apps store their data, according to the repository, which calls it the "direct successor" to an earlier exploit, bad_query. There have been no confirmed reports of crypto being stolen using the flaw.

Apple released iOS 27.2.1 on Monday, a day after the proof of concept was published. Its security notes list fixes for nine vulnerabilities, including an authorization issue in App Intents through which "an app may be able to access sensitive user data," as well as several Bluetooth and iMessage flaws.

However, the notes do not mention worse_query or its researcher, and Apple has not publicly confirmed whether the update addresses the flaw Binance described. At least one security expert believes it does. 23pds, chief information security officer at blockchain security firm SlowMist, urged users to update to iOS 27.2.1, saying in a tweet that the update would block the worse_query sandbox escape, along with other threats.

It is not the first time attackers have targeted Apple devices. Earlier this year, Google warned of iOS malware that targeted crypto apps on unpatched devices, while in August, the Dutch national cyber agency said hackers were exploiting a flaw in Apple's macOS Screen Sharing feature to plant Monero miners on Macs.

How Nicolas Cage Really Feels About Ryan Gosling Playing Ghost Rider

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In September 2026, Nicholas Cage slammed the Marvel Cinematic Universe for becoming a "glorified WWE." But that doesn't mean he's displeased about Ryan Gosling preparing to take on a role that he previously played — Johnny Blaze, aka Ghost Rider — in Marvel Studios' upcoming movie "Ghost Rider."

Cage's "Ghost Rider" ended up flopping its way to a franchise back in 2007, with the actor returning for the 2012 sequel "Ghost Rider: Spirit of Vengeance." Despite seeing sort of okay returns at the box office, neither film was a hit with critics, and after "Spirit of Vengeance," Johnny Blaze was relegated to cinematic history as Marvel Studios pushed ahead with the MCU. But at San Diego Comic-Con 2026, the studio announced that Gosling is set to star in a new Ghost Rider film, with "Deadpool & Wolverine" director Shawn Levy overseeing the project. Meanwhile, Cage has nothing but nice things to say about that.

The actor spoke to The Hollywood Reporter at the premiere of his new sports biopic "Madden" (the trailer for which left us wondering how David O. Russell is still making movies). "Ryan's going to do his thing with it, and I'm looking forward to seeing it," Cage told the outlet. "Ryan knows how I feel about him as an actor. We've had nice conversations, and we stay in touch. I was happy for him and wished him best of luck on his adventures, and I look forward to seeing the movie."

2007's "Ghost Rider" was written and directed by Mark Steven Johnson and saw Nicholas Cage in the title role, a motorcycle stuntman who sells his soul and transforms into a flaming emissary of Hell. Peter Fonda played the villain Mephistopheles, Ryan Gosling's wife Eva Mendes was Cage's love interest Roxanne Simpson, and Sam Elliot played mentor Carter Slade/The Caretaker. Sadly, despite a decent cast, the film received pretty terrible reviews. It did, however, make $228.7 million at the box office on a $110 million budget, which was evidently enough for Sony Pictures to make a sequel. But 2012's "Ghost Rider: Spirit of Vengeance" was met with even more scathing reviews, and despite bringing in a reasonable $132.5 million in theaters on a $57 million budget, the film marked the end of Cage's run as the hero.

Now, Gosling is set to take on Ghost Rider for the character's MCU debut, and it remains to be seen what direction the movie will take. Cage previously told JoBlo that Johnson's film "should've been an R-rated movie," adding that David S. Goyer's original script was "still a movie that should be made." Obviously, Marvel Studios won't be using Goyer's screenplay, but fans have yet to get a truly dark Ghost Rider film, and Gosling's outing as the character could — and, if you ask Cage, should — be it.

For now, THR claims "Heated Rivalry" star Connor Storrie is in talks to play the film's villain. Rosamund Pike was also previously reported to be a top pick for the love interest in Gosling's "Ghost Rider," but for now, we'll have to wait for official news.

Marvel Studios' "Ghost Rider" hits theaters on July 28, 2028.

SHIB Price Tests Support While Burns Tick 49% Higher

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Shiba Inu (SHIB) » SHIB Price Tests Support While Burns Tick 49% Higher

Absolute numbers stay modest even though the popular canine coin saw a wider burning campaign this week.

The Shiba Inu coin (SHIB) has drifted toward the $0.00000426 area, a level that has become the immediate line of defense as broader crypto weakness keeps pressure on the token. Buyers are now testing whether they can absorb selling there or whether the next leg lower opens up.

SHIB has tracked the wider market’s soft tone in recent sessions, leaving the $0.00000426 zone as the nearest area of interest. A clean hold could give the token a base to stabilize.

A decisive break would leave traders looking for the next pocket of demand, particularly while major cryptocurrencies remain under pressure.

$SHIB / USDT 🐕🔥SHIB is printing a potential Double Bottom formation on the daily chart! 👀Price is retesting the $0.0000043–$0.0000045 support zone, with buyers attempting to defend this level. A successful reversal could signal a shift in momentum.🚀 A breakout above… pic.twitter.com/Oo8aMEiLLW

This Shiba Inu (SHIB) price level matters less as a long-term pivot and more as a short-term filter.

Hold it and the near-term downside is at least temporarily limited. Lose it and the path of least resistance stays lower until broader sentiment improves.

SHIB burn trackers recorded a 49.21% rise in activity over the past seven days, with the chart showing a clear peak around November 7-8 after several quiet days.

Total SHIB sent to burn addresses now stands at roughly 410.845 trillion, or 41.08% of the original 1 quadrillion supply, across more than 21,900 transactions.

The 49% percentage jump looks sharp on the seven-day window, yet recent weekly totals have remained in the tens of millions of SHIB (worth only a few hundred dollars at current prices).

Most of the lifetime burn total still dates to the project’s early years. The recent uptick adds a small deflationary note but does not yet move the needle on circulating supply in any material way.

Shiba Inu’s near-term direction continues to depend more on overall crypto sentiment than on token-specific developments. Meme-linked assets tend to see stronger participation when risk appetite is high; when the market is cautious, buyers have less room to push against selling.

For now, the $0.00000426 area is the practical marker. Holding it would show that some demand is still present even in a soft tape. Losing it would reinforce that sellers remain in control while the wider market stays under pressure.

How forgotten Celtic and PSG star nearly joined Rangers as lid lifted on 'advanced negotiatons'

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Former Celtic midfielder Youssouf Mulumbu has revealed how he came very close to joining Rangers after holding “advanced negotiations” with the Ibrox club.

Mulumbu joined Celtic from Kilmarnock in 2018 and spent a year at the club, making a handful of appearances. However, the DR Congo internationalist could easily have been representing the other side of the Old Firm.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

“Football is crazy,” said Mulumbu. “I'll tell you the story of how I signed for Celtic - I was actually in advanced negotiations with Rangers.

“We were finalising the contract when negotiations suddenly stalled out of nowhere. I returned to France, and out of the blue, Brendan Rodgers called me personally. He spoke to me at length about the club and how he planned to integrate me.

“Looking back, I should have shown more patience. Instead of asking to go out on loan the moment I wasn't starting, I should have stayed, fought for my place in training, and waited for my opportunity. With hindsight, had I stayed put, I would have played far more games for Celtic.

“Moving to Celtic was a dream because I had always wanted to play for an elite club competing for silverware. The dynamic was very different from Kilmarnock, though. Celtic already had established midfield leaders, Scott Brown, Callum McGregor, and Nir Bitton.

“Forcing my way into that starting lineup was difficult. We had dropped into the Europa League after missing out on Champions League qualification, but Brendan Rodgers already had a settled tactical structure and preferred starting eleven.

“It was tough to break through, but playing at Celtic Park in front of those passionate fans was a special experience. In hindsight, I feel I could and should have done more to secure regular minutes.”

Former PSG youth Mulumbu, who also had spells at Norwich City and West Brom, had a far more fruitful time at Kilmarnock under Steve Clarke, who he worked with at the Baggies.

“Kilmarnock was incredible, you know,” the 39-year-old continued. I arrived there after a bad spell at Norwich where I had lost the passion for football. But I spoke with Steve Clarke at the time, and he told me to come and enjoy my football.

“Obviously, it's not the same wages and it's not the same facilities, but I was surprised because when I arrived there, I really enjoyed training every day and playing against good sides like Celtic, Rangers, and Aberdeen. And I discovered a country as well, you know. Different mentality, different culture. It was very, very good.

“I had played five games when I arrived, and after that, I was supposed to be transferred to Bordeaux in France. I remember I spoke with Steve Clarke and I spoke with the president at the time, and they were really sad because I was doing well. And the transfer failed.”

Adding in an interview with Paris Match Casino, Mulumbu went on: “As the transfer failed on the Friday, we then played on Sunday against Celtic and I scored! All right, yeah, we won 1–0, for the story! And no, that was very, very nice mentally, you know, because after what happened at Norwich, to come back as a football player and to play for a team that loves you, where you can feel the love of the supporters and of your teammates, that was great.”

Analysts project Bitcoin to move just 2.5% on today’s CPI data

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The historical source estimated a CPI-window move, not a cash hedge premium or a directional forecast. Its volatility readings are not live quotes.

A pre-release Bitcoin options analysis estimated a 2.5% move for a window containing the November 10 US inflation report. Its comparison of past and expected volatility did not establish whether buying protection was cheap.

The analysis reports 3,183 BTC of long liquidations, approximately $245 million, on November 5 and another 2,450 BTC, approximately $181 million, on November 8. These are forced closures of bullish Bitcoin positions, rather than totals for the entire crypto market.

The study puts Bitcoin’s 30-day realized-volatility reading at 33.7 and Deribit’s DVOL index at 40, leaving a reported gap of 6.3 volatility points. These are the study’s readings, not live quotes. It does not document the realized measure’s annualization method, limiting how precisely it can be compared with annualized DVOL.

The observations concern the period before October’s Consumer Price Index release was scheduled for November 10 at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, or 13:30 UTC. They are not a post-release market update.

Realized volatility describes swings that have already happened. Deribit defines DVOL as a forward-looking, annualized measure covering the next 30 days. Its calculation draws on option prices at expiries on either side of that horizon and interpolates between them.

The two readings describe different stretches of time. A turbulent week can lift the backward-looking measure and bring it closer to implied volatility without demonstrating that the cash price of protection has fallen.

The Daily Digits’ November 10 CryptoQuant study compares measures that look in opposite directions.

The reported gap alone cannot establish whether a particular protective option is cheap.

No exact quote time is supplied. The diagram does not forecast future swings.

Thirty markers per side represent the reported trailing and forward 30-day horizons, not prices or exact quote endpoints. The source reports 33.7, 40.03 and a 6.33-point difference. Its realized-volatility annualization method is not documented, limiting comparability. No values are recalculated. The divider is undated because the study gives no exact quote timestamp.

No exact quote time is supplied. The diagram does not forecast future swings.

DVOL condenses option pricing into a single index reading. It does not give the cash premium for a particular protective option, so it cannot alone tell a Bitcoin holder what buying protection through CPI would cost.

The Daily Digits separately estimates a 2.5% Bitcoin move, approximately $1,884, for a window containing CPI. It reports forward implied volatility of 47.9% for that window versus 38.9% afterward. The study does not disclose the exact expiry endpoints or quote timestamp, so that estimate cannot serve as a current, reproducible protection quote.

That event-window estimate also differs from DVOL’s constant 30-day horizon. Combining the two would blur the distinction between pricing around one release and pricing a month of uncertainty.

Neither measure supplies a directional forecast. Deribit’s explanation allows for large Bitcoin moves upward as well as downward, and an implied move sets no boundary on the eventual outcome.

HUNGER launches in Early Access on December 3

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Multiplayer extraction RPG HUNGER will launch in Early Access for PC via Steam on December 3, developer Good Fun Corporation announcd. An open beta test will run from November 27 to 29.

Here is an overview of the game, via its Steam page:

HUNGER throws you into the chaos of plague-ravaged Napoleonic Europe. This multiplayer extraction RPG blends brutal melee and ranged combat with deep long term character progression, questing and crafting. Scavenge for resources, battle unspeakable horrors, and outlast rival Living.

War has ravaged Europe—but something far worse now grips the continent. A Bacterium of unknown origin has twisted the land, leaving behind only ruin, fear, and something far more sinister. In this multiplayer first person extraction RPG, players will scavenge a decaying world, face unspeakable horrors, and outlast rival survivors in desperate, high-risk expeditions where every step could be your last…

At its core, this is a deep, first-person RPG—one where your story unfolds through the high-stakes loop of a multiplayer extraction shooter. Progress from level one to 100 by completing quests, surviving Expeditions, and mastering a robust skill system that evolves with your choices. The extraction loop isn’t just a gameplay feature—it’s how you grow stronger, build your reputation, and gain access to advanced weapons, rare gear, bags, armor, cosmetics, ingredients, and other vital resources. Every Expedition shapes your path forward.

Players can also choose a unique crafting or gathering profession, allowing them to create valuable items—whether for personal use, to aid their friends, or to sell within the player-driven marketplace. Crafting adds a layer of economic and strategic depth, rewarding specialization and community interdependence.

Whether you venture alone or in duos or trios, survival demands strategy, nerve, and adaptability. The game features no wipe mechanics typical of the extraction shooter genre, ensuring your progress and story persist. Customize your appearance with cosmetics found as loot in-game, trade in a player-driven marketplace, and connect, quest, and transact with others in the social hub known as the Chateau. Proximity VOIP adds an immersive edge to every encounter—friend, foe, or something in between.

As players progress, the endgame expands the core extraction loop with distinct player-versus-player and player-versus-environment experiences—including dungeons, boss encounters, and intense player-versus-player combat—offering ever-evolving challenges for those who survive long enough to face them.

Prepare yourself for a world where death is not the worst fate.

In the unforgiving World After the End, your survival and prosperity are intertwined with the denizens of the Chateau—a broad array of quest givers, shopkeepers, stashkeepers, bounty hunters, and other Living. Completing quests and building your reputation broadens your access to new locations, abilities, characters, weapons, gear, and vital resources.

Deliver fire with a rifle or pistol, or lob explosive glass grenades with an experimental hand mortar. When the ammunition runs dry or the gap is closing too fast to reload, draw your axe or mace to enter lethal hand-to-hand combat. HUNGER is built upon a mix of methodical and frenetic ranged and melee combat as you enter a deadly and brutal dance with both the Hunger and other Living who seek to take what is yours. Find and equip armor, bags, trinkets, medicines, weapons, and shields to ready yourself for the different environments and battles ahead.

While much of your equipment and weaponry may be lost, all experience contributes to progressing your Living and their level. Each of the Living has a set of unlockable unique Passive, Tactical, and Ultimate abilities, alongside deep and extremely customizable mastery trees with more than 100 talents to choose from. Build your Living for stealth and solo play by quieting your footsteps and lockpicking doors. Specialize for ranged combat by quickening your reload speed and increasing your weapon control. Sacrifice your lethality and support your party by repairing their armor and healing them. Survival is the only measure of success.

Explore forests, villages, monasteries, citadels, mines, and more as you and other Living attempt to explore and plunder the remains of the World After the End.

Linus Torvalds says that AI fixes drove most of the progress in latest Linux kernel

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It would certainly be a welcome development for open-source projects everywhere.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Most open-source projects have meager resources, and the inrush of AI-provided bug reports and "fixes" has been a bane on their respective maintainers. However, the Linux kernel is well-manned, and code for it generally has a higher barrier to entry than in other places. Linus Torvalds thinks that the flood of AI bug reports may be settling, as the kernel's Berkeley Packet Filter (BPF) portion saw more AI fixes than reports.

Torvalds splits off the changes among three areas: graphics, networking, and sound drivers; networking and the BPF; and miscellaneous changes. He proceeds to state that "much of this was driven by various AI fixes," an apparent underscoring of his stance that AI-written contributions should be embraced rather than shunned — so long as they're subject to the same quality control.

The Linux maestro also relayed a note from Alexei Starovoitov, the BPF maintainer, who claims that this subsection actually saw a higher rate of AI-provided fixes versus reports. This might signal a tidal shift, at least where the Linux kernel is concerned, and would be exceedingly welcome news for other open-source maintainers everywhere tired of dealing with junk reports at the same time they have to handle a higher influx of legitimate bugs, thanks to AI-driven bug discovery.

In Torvalds' own words, "I think most other areas haven't reached that point yet, but it's at least a sign that we may be starting to see the tide starting to turn."

Anyone can make a pointless bug report, but it's hard to deny that the existence of powerful AI bots has prompted a renewed focus on cybersecurity most everywhere. Firefox fixed 423 security bugs in an April release, Chrome found and fixed 1,072 bugs in June — making for more fixes in 2026 than the project's previous eight years — and my personal "favorite," NodeJS, sadly fixed 50 vulnerabilities instead of announcing its closure.

Many open-source projects have complained about high-volume, low-quality AI-driven bug reports, with some even shutting down their bug bounty programs altogether, or placing additional verifications for issue reports. We'd venture that the Linux kernel has a much larger bug-triaging team than your average project, though, meaning it has more resources to sift through the junk.

This tentative conclusion popped up in a message sent yesterday to the kernel mailing list, wherein Torvalds briefly describes the overall shape of the kernel's 7.4-rc2 update. He says that despite the update looking large from a pure file-count perspective, it's actually mostly composed of one- and two-line fixes and is "very straightforward" to implement.
